> og fra sorg — for du voldte at Ulvhild min døde et
> menløst barn —»
> Han vendte sig om, ravet indimot skigaren og faldt
> fremover den, med hodet ned paa armene graat han
> übændig, med lange, dype støn indimellem.
> Hustruen tok om hans skuldre:
> «Lavrans, Lavrans.» Men hun kunde ikke stagge
> ham. «Husbond.»
> «Aa aldrig, aldrig, aldrig skulde jeg git hende til
> den manden. Gud hjelpe mig — jeg har vel visst det
> hele tiden — han hadde brutt ned hendes ungdom og
> hendes fagre hæder. Jeg trodde det ikke, nei skulde
> jeg tro slik om Kristin — men jeg visste det endda.
> Endda saa er hun for god til den veke drengen som
> har ødt sig selv og hende — hadde han lokket hende
> ti ganger, jeg skulde ikke git hende til ham, saa han
> kan spilde mere av livet hendes og lykken —»
> «Hvad anden raad var der da,» sa moren opgit.
> «Du skjønner det jo du og, hun var alt hans —»
> «Ja jeg hadde ikke trængt at gjøre saa stort opstyr
> for at gi til Erlend det han alt hadde tat sig til selv,»
> sa Lavrans. «Det er en gjæv husbond hun har faat,
> Kristin min —.» Han rev i skigaren. Saa graat han
> igjen. Ragnfrid syntes, han var likesom blit litt mere
> ædru, men nu tok rusen overhaand igjen.
> Saa drukken og saa over sig fortvilet som manden
> var, syntes hun ikke, hun kunde dra ham op i aare
> stuen, der de skulde sove — der laa fuldt av gjester.
> Hun saa sig om — like ved var en liten laave, hvor de
> gjemte det gode høiet til hestene i vaaronnen. Hun gik
> bort og kek ind — der laa ingen; da tok hun og leiet
> manden ditind, lukket døren efter dem.
> Ragnfrid tok og karret høi op om sig og ham og
> lunet utenpaa med kapperne. Lavrans graat fra tid til
> anden og sa noget, men det var saa røret, at hun kunde
> ikke finde mening i det. Om en stund lettet hun hans
> hode op i fanget sit.
> «Kjære husbond min — nåar de nu har lagt slik en
> elskov til hinanden — kanhænde blir alt bedre end vi
> tror —»
> Lavrans svarte støtvis — syntes nu igjen klarere:
> «Skjønner du ikke — han har nu aldeles magten
> over hende, han som aldrig har kunnet raade sig selv.
> Vanskelig vil hun faa mot til at sætte sig imot noget
> hendes husbond vil — og maa hun det en dag, da vil
> hun pines saa bittert ved det selv — det milde barnet
> mit —.
> — Nu skjønner jeg snart ikke mere, hvorfor Gud
> lægger saa mange og tunge sorger paa mig — jeg har
> strævet trofast for at gjøre hans vilje. Hvorfor har han
> tat fra os børnene vore, Ragnfrid, ett efter ett — først
> sønnerne vore, saa liten Ulvhild, og nu har jeg git hende
> som jeg hadde kjærest, uten hæder, til en utryg og
> uforstandig mand. Nu har vi bare den lille tilbake
> >
> og det tykkes mig uklokt nu at glæde mig over hende,
> før jeg ser hvordan det gaar med hende, Ramborg.»
> Ragnfrid skalv som et løv. Da tok manden om hendes
> skulder:
> «Læg dig ned,» bad han, «saa sover vi —» og med
> hodet mot hustruens arm laa han en stund, sukket av
> og til, men tilslut sovnet han.
> Det var kulmørkt i laaven endda, da Ragnfrid rørte
> paa sig — hun undret sig over at hun hadde sovet.
> Hun følte ut for sig med haanden; Lavrans sat op med
> hænderne flettet om knærne.
> «Er du vaaken alt,» spurte hun forundret. «Fryser
> du?»
> «Nei,» svarte han rusten i mælet, «men jeg kan ikke
> sovne igjen.»
> «Er det Kristin du tænker paa,» spurte moren. «Det
> kan da gaa bedre end vi tror, Lavrans,» sa hun igjen.
> «Ja det er det jeg tænker paa,» sa manden. «Jaja.
> Mø eller kone, hun kom da i brudeseng med den hun
> hadde lagt sin elsk til. Det gjorde hverken du eller
> jeg, min stakkars Ragnfrid.»
> Hustruen gav et dumpt, dypt støn — hun slængte sig
> ned paa siden i høiet. Lavrans flyttet en haand bort
> paa hendes skulder.
> «Men jeg har ikke kunnet,» sa han heftig og vaande
> fuldt. «Nei jeg kunde ikke — være slik som du vilde
> ha mig — da vi var unge. Jeg er ikke slik —»
> Om en stund sa Ragnfrid sagte og i graat:
> «Vi har jo levet godt sammen allikevel — Lavrans —
> alle disse aarene.»
> «Saa har jeg trodd selv,» svarte han mørkt.
> r
> Tankerne tumlet og trængte sig i ham. Det ene
> nøkne blikket som brudgom og brud hadde slaat mot
> hinanden i, de to unge aasyn som flammet op i rød
> lue — ham tyktes det være en skamløshet. Det hadde
> svidd ham, dette var hans datter. Men han blev ved
> at se de øinene — og vildt og blindt stred han imot, at
> dækket skulde bli revet fra noget i hans eget hjerte,
> han hadde aldrig villet vite, at det var der, han hadde
> verget sig mot sin egen hustru, da hun lette efter det —.
> Han hadde ikke kunnet, tok han haardt i for sig selv.
> I djævelens navn, han var blit giftet bort som gut, han
> hadde ikke valgt selv, hun var ældre end ham — han
> hadde ikke attraaet hende; han hadde ikke villet lære
> dette av hende — elskov. Han blev het av blu endda,
> nåar han tænkte paa det — at hun vilde hat ham til
> at elske hende, nåar han ikke hadde villet en slik kjær
> lighet av hende. At hun hadde budt ham alt dette som
> han aldrig hadde bedt om.
> Han hadde været hende en god husbond, det hadde
> han trodd selv. Han hadde vist hende al den ære han
> kunde, git hende fuld myndighet med sig og raadspurt
> hende i alt, været hende tro — og seks børn hadde de
> da hat. Han hadde bare villet faa lov til at leve med
> hende uten hun altid skulde gripe efter dette i hans
> hjerte som han selv ikke vilde blotte —.
> Ingen hadde han baaret elskov til. — Ingunn, Karls
> hustru paa Bru. Lavrans rødmet i kulmørket. Han
> hadde altid gjestet dem, nåar han reiste ned gjennem
> dalen. Han hadde vist end ikke talt med husfruen i
> enerum en gang. Men nåar han saa hende — bare han
> tænkte paa hende, sanset han noget som den første lugt
> av jorderne om vaaren, straks sneen er gaat bort. Han
> visste det nu, det kunde hændt ham ogsaa — han
> kunde ha elsket han og.
> Men han var blit gift saa ung, og han var blit sky.
> Saa var han kommet til at trives bedst ute i ødeskogen
> — indpaa vidden, hvor alle liv som lever vil ha vidt
> rum omkring sig, rum til at flygte gjennem, — sky
> speider de mot hver fremmed som vil snige sig ind
> paa dem —.
> En tid paa aaret var der da dyrene i skogen og paa
> tjeldet glemte skyheten sin. Da de bruste mot hunnen
> sin. Men han hadde faat sin til gave. Og hun hadde
> budt ham alt han ikke hadde beilet om —.
> Men ungerne i reden. — De hadde været den lille lune
> flekken i ødet, den inderste søteste lyst i hans liv. De
> smaa lyse pikehoderne under hans haand. —
> Gift, det var han blit — uspurt næsten. Venner —
> han hadde mange, og han hadde ingen. Krig — det
> hadde været glæde, men der blev ikke mere krig —
> hans rustning hang oppe i loftet, litet brukt. Han var
> blit bonde —. Men han hadde hat døttrene — alt han
> hadde levet var blit kjært, fordi han trygget dem med
> det, de fine bløte smaa livene han hadde holdt i hæn
> derne sine. Han husket Kristins lille toaars skrot paa
> skuldren sin, hendes linbleke, myke håar mot kinden
> sin. Hendes smaahænder som holdt i hans belte mens
> hun stanget sin runde, haarde barnepande mot hans
> skulderblade, nåar han red ut med hende bak sig paa
> hesten.
> Og nu hadde hun de samme hete øinene — og hun
> hadde faat sit. Hun sat der i halvskyggen op mot sen
> gens silkeputer. I kjerteskjæret var hun helt gylden —
> gylden krone og gylden serk og gyldent håar utover
> de nøkne gyldne arme. Hendes øine var ikke blyge
> mere —.
> Faren kved av skam.
> Og endda var det som hans hjerte sprang i blødning.
> For det han ikke selv hadde faat. Og for hustruen her
> ved sin side som han ikke hadde kunnet gi.
> Syk av medynk tok han i mørket efter Ragnfrids
> haand:
> «Ja jeg trodde vi levet godt sammen,» sa han. «Jeg
> trodde du sørget over børnene vore. — Ja og at du
> hadde et tungt sind. Aldrig tænkte jeg paa det, at det
> kunde være mig som ikke var dig en god husbond —»
> Ragnfrid skalv som i krampe:
> «Du var en god husbond altid, Lavrans.»
> «Hm.» Lavrans sat med haken helt ned paa knærne.
> «Endda hadde du kanske faat bedre om du var blit
> gift slik som datter vor idag —»
> Ragnfrid for op, skrek lavt og skjærende:
> «Vet du! Hvordan fik du vite — hvor længe har du
> visst —»
> «Jeg vet ikke hvad du taler om,» sa Lavrans om en
> stund, med en underlig knuget stemme.
> «Jeg taler om det, at jeg var ikke mø, da jeg blev
> din hustru,» svarte Ragnfrid, og hendes røst var klar
> og klinger av fortvilelse.
> Om en stund svarte Lavrans som før:
> «Det har jeg aldrig visst før nu.»
> Ragnfrid laa ned i høiet, skaket av graat. Da rien
> var over, løftet hun hodet litt. Der begyndte at sive litt
> graat lys ind gjennem gluggen i væggen. Hun kunde
> skimte sin mand, at han sat med armene slynget om
> knærne, urørlig som han var av sten.
> «Lavrans — tal til mig —» jamret hun.
> «Hvad vil du jeg skal si,» spurte han übevægelig.
> «Aa jeg vet ikke — at du skulde bande mig — slaa
> mig —»
> «Det vilde være noget sent nu,» svarte manden; der
> var som skyggen av et haansmil i hans stemme.
> Ragnfrid graat igjen. «Ja jeg agtet ikke paa det, at
> jeg sveg dig. Jeg syntes, saa sveget og saa krænket
> var jeg blit selv. Der hadde ingen spart mig. De kom
> med dig — jeg saa dig jo bare tre ganger før vi blev
> gifte — jeg syntes du var bare en gut, rød og hvit —
> saa ung og barnslig —»
> «Jeg var det,» sa Lavrans, og der kom litt mere
> klang i stemmen hans. «Og derfor vilde jeg trodd, at
> du som var kvinde, du vilde være mere ræd for at —
> for at svige en som var saa ung, at han intet skjønte —»
> «Slik kom jeg til at tænke siden,» sa Ragnfrid graa
> tende. «Da jeg var blit kjendt med dig. Snart nok
> kom den tiden, da jeg vilde git sjælen min tyve ganger
> om jeg kunde blit skyldfri mot dig.»
> Lavrans sat taus og urørlig; da sa hustruen igjen:
> «Du spørger ikke om noget?»
> «Hvad skulde det nytte til. Det var ham som — vi
> møtte likfærden hans paa Feginsbrekka, da vi bar Ulv
> hild ind til Nidaros —»
> «Ja,» sa Ragnfrid. «Vi maatte gaa ut av veien —
> ind paa engen. Jeg saa dem føre baaren hans forbi —
> med prester og munke og væbnede svende. Jeg hørte
> han hadde faat en god død — forlikt sig med Gud.
> Jeg bad, der vi stod med Ulvhilds baare mellem os, at
> min synd og min sorg maatte lægges for hans føtter
> paa den sidste dag —»
> «ja du gjorde vel det,» sa Lavrans, og der var den
> samme skyggen av haan i hans stille stemme.
> «Du vet ikke alt,» sa Ragnfrid, kold av fortvilelse.
> «Mindes du, at han kom ut til os paa Skog den første
> vintren vi var gifte —»
> «Ja,» svarte manden.
> «Da Bjørgulv droges med døden —. Aa der hadde
> ingen spart mig. Han var drukken, da han gjorde det
> mot mig — siden sa han, han hadde aldrig hat mig
> kjær, han vilde ikke ha mig — han bad mig glemme
> det —. Far min visste ikke dette, han har ikke sveget
> dig, det maa du aldrig tro. Men Trond — vi var hin
> andens kjæreste venner dengangen, jeg klaget det for
> ham. Han vilde true den manden til at egte mig —
> men han var bare gutten, han fik bank—. Siden raadet
> han mig til at tie med det og ta dig —»
> Hun sat stille litt.
> «Da han kom ut til Skog —. Der var gaat et aar;
> jeg tænkte ikke saa meget paa det. Men han kom
> ditut — han sa, at han angret, han vilde hat mig nu,
> om jeg hadde været ugift — han holdt av mig. Saa
> sa han. Gud maa dømme om han sa sandt. Da han
> var reist — jeg turde ikke gaa paa fjorden, jeg turde
> ikke for synden, ikke med barnet. Og saa hadde jeg —
> saa hadde jeg begyndt at holde slik av dig!» Hun
> skrek engang, som i vildeste pine. Manden vendte fort
> hodet mot hende.
> «Da Bjørgulv var født — aa jeg syntes jeg hadde ham
> kjærere end livet mit. Da han laa og droges med døden
> — jeg tænkte, slukner han, saa slukner jeg og. Men
> jeg bad ikke Gud spare guttens liv —»
> Lavrans sat meget længe før han spurte, dødt og tungt:
> «Var det for jeg var ikke far hans?»
> «Jeg visste ikke om du var det,» sa Ragnfrid stiv
> nende.
> Længe sat de dødsens stille begge to. Da spurte
> manden med ett heftig:
> «Jesu navn, Ragnfrid — hvorfor sier du mig dette
> — nu?»
> «Aa jeg vet ikke.» Hun vred sine hænder saa finger
> leddene knaket. «For at du kan faa hevn paa mig.
> Jag mig ut av gaarden din —»
> «Tænker du det skulde hjelpe mig —.» Hans stemme
> skalv av haan. «Saa er der døttrene vore,» sa han stille.
> «Kristin — og den lille.»
> Ragnfrid sat en stund.
> «Jeg mindes hvordan du dømte om Erlend Nikulaus
> søn,» sa hun sagte. «Hvad dømmer du da om mig —»
> Der gik en lang kuldegysning gjennem mandens
> legeme — løste ut litt av stivheten.
> «Du har nu — vi har nu levet sammen i syv og tyve
> aar — snart. Det er ikke slik som med en fremmed
> mand. Jeg skjønner det, at du har hat det værre end
> vondt.»
> Ragnfrid sank hulkende sammen under hans ord.
> Hun vaaget sig til at ta bort paa hans ene haand. Han
> rørte sig ikke, sat stille som en død. Da graat hun
> høiere og høiere — hendes husbond sat like übevæge
> lig og saa mot den gråa lysning omkring døren. Til
> slut laa hun som om al graat var rundet ut av hende.
> Da strøk han flygtig nedover hendes arm. Saa tok
> hun paa at graate igjen.
> «Mindes du,» sa hun indimellem graaten. «Den man
> den som kom til os engang, mens vi var paa Skog.
> 24 — Undset: Kristin.
> Han som kunde de gamle kvadene? Mindes du ett om
> en død mand som var kommet tilbake fra kvalverdenen
> og sa sønnen sin frasagn om det han hadde set? Da
> hørtes gny fra helvedes dypeste grund, til sine mænd
> malte utro kvinder muld for mat. Blodige var stenene
> de drog, blodige hang hjerterne ut av deres bryst —»
> Lavrans sa intet.
> «Jeg har tænkt i alle disse aarene paa de ord,» sa
> Ragnfrid. «Hver dag var det som hjertet mit blødde,
> for hver dag syntes jeg, at jeg malte dig muld til’ mat —»
> Lavrans visste ikke selv, hvorfor han svarte som han
> gjorde. Han syntes han var tom og hul i brystet som en
> mand, de har ristet blodørn paa ryggen. Men han Ia
> haanden tungt og træt paa sin hustrus hode og mælte:
> «Muld maa der vel males, Ragnfrid min, før maten
> kan gro.»
> Da hun vilde ta hans haand og kysse den, rev han
> den heftig til sig. Saa saa han ned paa hustruen, tok
> hendes ene haand og la den paa sit knæ, lutet sit kolde
> og stivnede ansigt ned imot den. Og slik blev de sit
> tende, uten at røre sig og uten at tale mere sammen.


## English translation supplied to the annotator

> Lavrans and his guests were therefore in good spirits when they went down to greet the almsfolk in the hearth-room.
> The poor sat around the table, eating milk porridge and drinking good ale with it, and Kristin went about the table, serving the old and the sick.
> Lavrans greeted his guests and asked whether they were content with the fare. Then he went over and welcomed an old pauper who had been brought to Jørundgaard that day. The man’s name was Haakon; he had been a warrior under old King Haakon and had accompanied the king on his last expedition to Scotland. He was now utterly destitute and nearly blind; people had offered to settle him in a cottage, but he preferred to be taken from farm to farm, for everywhere he was received more as an honored guest, because he possessed unusually great knowledge and had seen much of the world.
> Lavrans stood with his hand on his brother’s shoulder—Aasmund Bjørgulfsøn had come to visit Jørundgaard. He too asked Haakon how well pleased he was with the food.
> “The ale is good, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn,” said Haakon. “But a mare must have cooked our porridge today. A bedridden cook makes scorched porridge to go with the meal, says the proverb, and this porridge is scorched.”
> “That was ill done,” said Lavrans, “that I should give you scorched porridge. But the old saying does not always hold true, I hope, for it was my daughter who cooked the porridge herself.” He laughed and told Kristin and Tordis to make haste and bring in the platters of meat.
> Kristin slipped quickly out and across toward the cookhouse. Her heart was pounding—she had caught a glimpse of her uncle’s face when Haakon said that about the cook and the porridge.
> Late that evening she saw her father and uncle walking up and down the courtyard for a long time, talking. She was dizzy with fear, and it did not make matters better when she noticed the next day that her father was taciturn and unhappy. But he said nothing.
> Nor did he say anything after her uncle had left. But Kristin noticed that he did not speak with Haakon as much as he used to, and when the time for keeping the old man was over, Lavrans did not offer to retain him for another term, but let him be moved to the next farm.
> Otherwise there were reasons enough for Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn to be unhappy and downcast that summer, for it now plainly threatened to be a year of famine in the district, and the farmers called an assembly to deliberate on how they should meet the approaching winter. By late summer it was already clear to most of them that they would have to slaughter or drive south for sale the greater part of their herds and buy grain to bring home as winter food for the people. The year before had not been a good grain year, so the stores of old grain were smaller than usual.
> One morning at the beginning of autumn, Ragnfrid went out with all three of her daughters to look at some linen she had lying on the bleaching ground. Kristin praised her mother’s weaving highly. Then Ragnfrid stroked Ramborg’s hair:
> “This shall go into your bridal chest, little one.”
> “Mother,” said Ulvhild, “am I not to have any chest, then, when I go into the convent?”
> “You know you will have no smaller dowry from home than your sisters,” said Ragnfrid. “But they will not be the same things that you need. And you know well enough that you are to stay with your father and me as long as we live—if you wish it.”
> “And when you enter the convent,” said Kristin, uncertainly, “perhaps, Ulvhild, I shall already have been a nun for many years.”
> She looked across at her mother, but Ragnfrid was silent.
> “If I had been such that I could have married,” said Ulvhild, “then I would never have turned away from Simon—he was kind, and he was so sorrowful when he bade farewell to us all.”
> “You know your father has said we are not to speak of this,” said Ragnfrid, but Kristin said defiantly:
> “Yes, I know he grieved more at parting from you than from me.”
> Her mother said angrily:
> “Then he would have had to be rather proud to show you that he grieved—you did not deal honorably with Simon Andressøn, my daughter. Even so, he begged us not to threaten or curse you—”
> “No, he must have thought he had cursed me so much,” said Kristin in the same manner, “that no one else needed to tell me how wretched I am. But I never noticed that Simon cared greatly for me until he understood that I held another man dearer than him.”
> “Go home, you two,” said their mother to the little ones. She sat down on a log lying there and drew Kristin down beside her.
> “You surely know,” she began, “that it has always been held most seemly and honorable for a man not to speak too much of love to his betrothed—not to sit alone with her or show himself too ardent—”
> “Oh, I wonder,” said Kristin, “whether young people who hold one another dear do not forget themselves now and then, but always remember what the old deem seemly.”
> “Take care, Kristin,” said her mother, “that you never forget it.” She sat for a while. “It is so, I understand, that your father fears you have bestowed your love upon a man to whom he would be loath to give you.”
> “What has my uncle said?” Kristin asked after a little while.
> “Nothing except,” said her mother, “that Erlend of Husaby has a better lineage than reputation. Yes, he has apparently spoken to Aasmund and asked him to put in a word for him with Lavrans. Your father was little pleased when he heard this.”
> But Kristin sat radiant. Erlend had spoken to her uncle. She who had suffered so because he had sent no word.
> Then her mother said again:
> “Now it is so that Aasmund mentioned something about a rumor said to have gone around Oslo, that this Erlend was supposed to have loitered in the lanes around the nunnery and that you went out and spoke with him by the fences.”
> “Well?” asked Kristin.
> “Aasmund counsels this match, you understand,” said Ragnfrid. “But then Lavrans grew angrier than I remember ever having seen him. He said that a suitor who took that road to his daughter would find him with sword in hand. Dishonorable enough was the way we had dealt with the people of Dyfrin, but if Erlend had enticed you to run about the roads with him in the dark, and that while you were in a convent, then he took it as ample proof that you were better served by losing such a husband.”
> Kristin clenched her hands in her lap—the color came and went in her face. Her mother put an arm around her waist—but Kristin twisted away and screamed, beside herself with rebellion:
> “Leave me be, Mother! Perhaps you want to feel whether I have grown thicker around the waist—”
> The next moment she stood up with her hand against her cheek—bewildered, she looked down into her mother’s blazing face. No one had struck her since she was a little child.
> “Sit down,” said Ragnfrid. “Sit down,” she repeated, and her daughter obeyed. Her mother sat silent for a while; when she spoke, her voice was uncertain:
> “I have seen it, Kristin: you have never held me very dear. I thought perhaps it was because you believed I did not love you as much—not as your father loves you. I let it be—I thought that when the time came for you yourself to bear a child, then you would surely understand—.
> “I was still suckling you, and already it was so that whenever Lavrans came near us two, you let my breast fall from your mouth and reached toward him and laughed so that my milk ran over your lips. Lavrans thought this amusing—and God knows I did not begrudge him that; I did not begrudge you that, or that your father played and laughed whenever he saw you. I myself thought it a pity for you, poor little life, that I could not cease all my weeping. I thought more about whether I should lose you too than I rejoiced that I had you. But God and the Virgin Mary know that I did not love you any less than Lavrans did.”
> Tears ran down Ragnfrid’s cheeks, but her face was quite calm, and so was her voice:
> “God knows I never bore him or you any grudge for the friendship between you. I thought I had not given him too much joy in the years we had lived together; I was glad that he had you. And I thought too, if Ivar, my father, had been like that toward me—.
> “There are many things, Kristin, that a mother ought to teach her daughter to guard against. I thought it was not needed with you, who have followed your father all these years—you should know what is honorable and right. That which you mentioned just now—do you think I could imagine that you would wish to cause Lavrans such sorrow—.
> “I only wished to tell you—I desire that you may have a husband whom you can love. But then you must conduct yourself wisely—not let Lavrans get the thought that you have chosen a man of misfortune, one who does not respect women’s peace or honor. For he will not give you to such a man—not even if it were a matter of freeing you from open shame. Lavrans would sooner let steel decide the right between himself and the man who had ruined your life—.”
> With that, her mother rose and left her.
> 11.
> On Saint Bartholomew’s Mass Day, the twenty-fourth of August, the blessed King Haakon’s daughter’s son was acclaimed at the Haugating. Among the men sent there from northern Gudbrandsdal was Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn. He had been the king’s man since his youth, but in all those years had seldom come near the court, and he had never tried to turn to his advantage the good name he had won in the campaign against Duke Eirik. Nor was he eager to travel to the assembly of acclamation, but he could not refuse. The delegates from the northern valley had also been charged with trying to purchase grain in the south of the country and send it by ship to Raumsdal.
> The people in the districts were despondent and afraid of the approaching winter. The farmers also thought it ill that a child should once again become king of Norway. Old people remembered the time when King Magnus had died and his sons were children, and Sira Eirik said:
> “Væ terræ, übi puer rex est. In the Norwegian tongue that means: there is no rest at night for rats on a farm where the cat is young.”
> Ragnfrid Ivarsdatter managed the farm while her husband was away, and it was good both for Kristin and for her that their heads and hands were full of care and work. Throughout the district people struggled to gather moss in the mountains and strip bark, for there had been little hay and almost no straw, and even the foliage gathered later than Saint John’s Mass had yellowed and was poor. On Holy Cross Day, when Sira Eirik carried the crucifix over the fields, many in the procession wept and loudly begged God to have mercy on people and livestock.
> About a week after Holy Cross Day, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn came home from the assembly.
> It was long past people’s bedtime, but Ragnfrid was still sitting in the weaving room. She had so much to attend to during the days now that she often worked late into the evenings at weaving and sewing. Ragnfrid also liked this house very much. It was said to be the oldest room on the farm; they also called it the dyngje, and people said it had stood there since heathen times. Kristin and the maid named Astrid were with her; they sat spinning by the hearth.
> They had sat silent and sleepy for a while when they heard the hoofbeats of a single horse—a man came riding at great speed into the wet courtyard. Astrid went into the vestibule and looked out—soon afterward she returned, followed by Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn.
> Both his wife and daughter saw at once that he was rather drunk. He staggered and seized the smoke-vent pole while Ragnfrid relieved him of his soaking-wet cloak and hat and unbuckled his sword belt.
> “Where have you left Halvdan and Kolbein?” she said, a little frightened. “Did you ride away from them on the road?”
> “No, I rode away from them at Loptsgaard,” he said, and laughed a little. “I had such a desire to come home—I can get no peace until— They went to bed down there, but then I took Guldsveinen and slipped home—”
> “You must find me a little food, Astrid,” he said to the maid. “Bring it in here, so you need not walk so far in the rain. But be quick; I have eaten nothing since early today—”
> “Did you get no food at Loptsgaard?” his wife asked in surprise.
> Lavrans sat on the bench, rocking himself and laughing a little:
> “You know there was food—but I had no appetite while I was there. I drank with Sigurd for a while—but—then I thought I might as well go home at once as wait until tomorrow—”
> Astrid came with ale and food; she had also brought dry shoes for the master.
> Lavrans fumbled as he tried to take off his spurs, but nearly toppled forward.
> “Come here, Kristin,” he begged, “and help your father. I know you do it from a loving heart—a loving heart, yes—today.”
> Kristin obeyed and knelt down. Then he took her head in both hands and lifted it:
> “You surely know, my daughter—I wish nothing but your good. I would not cause you sorrow unless I saw that I was sparing you many sorrows afterward. You are still very young, Kristin—you turned seventeen this year—three days after Saint Halvard’s Mass—seventeen years old you are—”
> Kristin had finished serving him. A little pale, she rose and sat down again on her stool by the hearth.
> Lavrans’s drunkenness seemed to lessen somewhat as he grew full. He answered his wife’s and the serving maid’s questions about the assembly—yes, it had been splendid. They had managed to purchase grain and some flour and malt, some in Oslo and some in Tunsberg; they were foreign wares, could have been better, but could also have been worse. Yes, he had met many kinsmen and acquaintances and brought greetings home. But he sat letting the answers drip from him.
> “I spoke with Sir Andres Gudmundsøn,” he said after Astrid had gone out. “Simon has drunk his betrothal ale with the young widow at Manvik. The wedding is to be held at Dyfrin around Saint Andrew’s Mass. The boy has decided for himself this time. I kept away from Sir Andres in Tunsberg, but he sought me out—wanted to tell me that he knew for certain Simon saw Lady Halfrid for the first time around midsummer this year. He feared I might think Simon had had this rich marriage in mind when he broke with us.” Lavrans sat for a while and laughed mirthlessly. “You understand, this honorable man was very much afraid we might think such a thing of his son.”
> Kristin breathed more easily. She thought this was what had upset her father so. Perhaps all along he had hoped that the marriage between Simon Andressøn and her might come about after all. At first she had feared he had learned something about her conduct in Oslo.
> She rose and bade them good night. Then her father told her to wait a little longer.
> “I have one more piece of news,” said Lavrans. “I could have kept silent about it, Kristin—but it is better that you know. It is this: you must try to forget the man upon whom you have set your heart.”
> Kristin had been standing with her arms hanging and her head bowed. Now she looked up into her father’s face. She moved her lips, but no audible sound came out.
> Lavrans looked away from his daughter’s gaze; he flung out his hand:
> “You surely know that I would not oppose it if I could truly believe it would be for your good.”
> “What news did you hear on this journey, Father?” said Kristin, her voice clear.
> “Erlend Nikulaussøn and his kinsman Sir Munan Baardsøn came to me in Tunsberg,” Lavrans replied. “Sir Munan asked for you on Erlend’s behalf, and I answered him no.”
> Kristin stood for a while, breathing heavily.
> “Why will you not give me to Erlend Nikulaussøn?” she asked.
> “I do not know how much you know about the man whom you would have as husband,” said Lavrans. “If you cannot understand the reason yourself, it will not be pleasant for you to hear it from my mouth.”
> “Is it because he has been excommunicated and outlawed?” Kristin asked as before.
> “Do you know what caused King Haakon to banish his close kinsman from his court—and that in the end he came under the ban because he defied the archbishop’s command—and that he did not leave the country alone?”
> “Yes,” said Kristin. Her voice grew uncertain. “I know too that he was eighteen when he became acquainted with her—his concubine.”
> “I was that age when I married,” Lavrans replied. “When I was young, we reckoned that at eighteen a man could answer for himself and govern his own welfare and that of others.”
> Kristin stood silent.
> “You called that woman his concubine, the one with whom he lived for ten years and had children,” said Lavrans after a while. “Little pleased would I be on the day I sent my daughter from home with a husband who had lived openly in concubinage year after year before he married. But you know it was not concubinage.”
> “You did not judge Lady Aashild and Sir Bjørn so harshly,” said Kristin softly.
> “Even so, I cannot say that I would gladly see us become related by marriage to them,” Lavrans replied.
> “Father,” said Kristin, “have you then been so sinless all your life that you dare judge Erlend so harshly—”
> “God knows,” Lavrans replied roughly, “that I judge no man to be a greater sinner before Him than I am myself. But one cannot reckon matters so that I must give away my daughter to any man who pleases to ask for her merely because we all need God’s mercy.”
> “You know I did not mean it like that,” said Kristin vehemently. “Father—Mother—you too were young—do you not remember enough to know that it is not easy to guard against the sin that love causes—”
> Lavrans turned bloodred.
> “No,” he said shortly.
> “Then you do not know what you are doing,” Kristin screamed in despair, “if you part Erlend Nikulaussøn and me!”
> Lavrans sat down again on the bench.
> “You are only seventeen, Kristin,” he began again. “It may be that he and you—that you have grown dearer to each other than I thought. But he is not so young a man that he should not have understood—had he been a good man, he would not have approached such a young, dependent child as you are with words of love.—That you were promised to another must have seemed a small matter to him—.
> “But I will not marry my daughter to a man who has two children with another man’s lawful wife. Do you know that he has children?
> “You are too young to understand that such a wrong breeds strife—and unrest in a lineage—without end. The man cannot betray his own offspring, nor can he set it right—he will have difficulty finding a way to advance his son among people or to marry his daughter to any man other than a servant or a small farmer. Those children would have to be without flesh and blood if they did not come to hate you and your children—.
> “Do you not understand, Kristin—such sins—God may forgive such sins more readily than many others, but they waste a lineage in a way that can never be remedied. I thought of Bjørn and Aashild too—there stood this Munan, her son, dressed in gold, sitting in the council of the king’s councillors; he and his brothers have their maternal inheritance, and he has not greeted his mother in her poverty all these years. Yes, that is the man your friend chose as his spokesman.
> “No, I say, no. You shall not enter that lineage while my head is above the earth.”
> Kristin struck her hands over her face and burst into tears:
> “Then I shall pray to God night and day, night and day, that if you do not change your mind, He must take me away from here!”
> “It is useless to speak more of this tonight,” said her father, tormented. “You may not believe it, but I must govern you as I think I can answer for it. Go to rest now, child.”
> He held out his hand to her, but she would not look at it and went sobbing out of the room.
> The parents remained seated for a while. Then Lavrans said to his wife:
> “Will you fetch a drink of ale—no, bring in some wine,” he asked. “I am tired—.”
> Ragnfrid did as he asked. When she returned with the tall goblet, her husband sat with his face buried in his hands. He looked up, glanced over her headdress and down her arms:
> “Poor thing, now you are wet.—Drink to me, then, Ragnfrid.”
> She barely touched her mouth to the goblet.
> “No, drink with me now,” said Lavrans vehemently, and tried to pull his wife down onto his lap. Reluctantly she yielded to him. Lavrans said:
> “You will stand with me in this matter, my wife? It is best for Kristin herself that she understand from the outset that she must put this man out of her mind.”
> “It will be hard for the child,” said her mother.
> “Yes, I understand that,” Lavrans replied.
> They sat for a while; then Ragnfrid asked:
> “What does he look like, this Erlend of Husaby?”
> “Oh,” said Lavrans, drawing out the word. “A handsome fellow he is—in a way. But he does not look fit for much other than beguiling women.”
> They were silent again for a while; then Lavrans said once more:
> “The great inheritance he received after Sir Nikulaus he has apparently managed in such a way that it has greatly diminished. It was not for such a son-in-law that I have labored and sought to secure my children.”
> The mother paced restlessly up and down the floor. Lavrans continued:
> “What I liked least was that he tried to entice Kolbein with silver—he was to carry a secret letter from him to Kristin.”
> “Did you look in the letter?” asked Ragnfrid.
> “No, I did not care to,” said Lavrans curtly. “I handed it back to Sir Munan and told him what I thought of such conduct. He had attached his seal too—I do not know what one is to say of such childish tricks. Sir Munan showed me the seal, that it was King Skule’s privy seal which Erlend possesses after his father. He must have thought I would be reminded that it is a great honor they are seeking my daughter. But I think Sir Munan would not have pursued this matter for Erlend with such warmth if he did not understand that with this man the power and honor of the Husaby lineage, won in the days of Sir Nikulaus and Sir Baard, are declining—Erlend can no longer expect to make such a marriage as his birth entitled him to.”
> Ragnfrid stopped before her husband:
> “Now I do not know, husband, whether you are right in this matter. First it must be said that, the times being as they are, many men on the great estates have had to content themselves with less power and honor than their fathers before them. You know best yourself that it is less easy for a man to gain riches either from land or from trade than it was formerly in the world—”
> “I know, I know,” the man interrupted impatiently. “All the more reason to govern thoughtfully what one has inherited—”
> But his wife continued:
> “Then there is also this to say: it does not seem to me that Kristin would be an unequal match for Erlend. In Sweden your lineage is among the best; your grandfather and your father bore the name of knight in this country. My forefathers were landed men, son after father, for many hundreds of years until old Ivar; my father and my grandfather were royal governors. It is true that neither you nor Trond has received titles or land under the Crown. But then it seems to me one might say that Erlend Nikulaussøn stands no differently than you.”
> “It is not the same,” said Lavrans vehemently. “Power and the name of knight lay ready to Erlend’s hand, and he turned away from them for the sake of adultery. But now I see that you too are against me. Perhaps, like Aasmund and Trond, you think it is an honor for me that these great lords wish to have my daughter for one of their kinsmen—”
> “I have told you,” said Ragnfrid, somewhat heatedly, “that I do not think you need be so sensitive that you fear Erlend’s kinsmen will think they are stooping in this matter. But do you not understand from everything—the gentle, compliant child had the courage to set herself against us and reject Simon Darre—have you not seen that Kristin has been utterly unlike herself since she came from Oslo? Do you not see that she goes about as though she had come out from under the mountain—.
> “Do you not understand that she holds this man so dear that if you do not yield, a great misfortune may happen here?”
> “What do you mean by that?” asked her father, looking sharply up.
> “Many a man greets his son-in-law without knowing it,” said Ragnfrid.
> It was as though the man stiffened; slowly his face turned white:
> “You who are her mother!” he said hoarsely. “Have you—have you seen—such certain signs—that you dare accuse your own daughter of this—”
> “No, no,” said Ragnfrid quickly. “I did not mean what you think. But no one can know what has happened or what may happen. She has no thought except that she loves this man—I have seen that—one day she might show us that she holds him dearer than honor—or life!”
> Lavrans sprang up:
> “Oh, you are mad! Can you think such things of our fair, good child! She can hardly have gone far where she was—among the nuns. She is not a dairy wench, I should think, who lies down behind a split-rail fence. You must understand that she cannot have seen this man or spoken with him very many times—it will surely pass; it is surely only a young maiden’s fancy.
> “God knows it seems heavy enough to me to see her grieve so, but you must know this will pass with time!
> “Life, you say, and honor—. Here at home on my own farm I can surely guard my maiden. Nor do I believe any maiden of good lineage, reared in Christianity and chastity, parts so quickly with either honor or life. Hah, that is the sort of thing people make ballads about—but I think it is so that when a man or maiden is tempted to do such a thing, they make a ballad about it and are helped by that, but refrain from doing it—
> “You yourself,” he said, stopping before his wife. “There was another whom you would rather have had at the time you and I were joined. What sort of lot do you think you would have had if your father had let you decide that matter—?”
> Now it was Ragnfrid who had turned deathly pale:
> “Jesus, Mary! Who told you—”
> “Sigurd at Loptsgaard said something about it—it was just after we had moved here to the valley,” said Lavrans. “But answer what I asked—do you think you would have been happier if Ivar had given you to that man?”
> His wife stood with her head deeply bowed.
> “That man,” she answered almost inaudibly, “did not want me.” A tremor passed through her body—she struck out into the air with a clenched hand.
> Then her husband cautiously laid his hands on her shoulders.
> “Is that it?” he asked, overwhelmed, and a deep and sorrowful wonder broke into his voice. “Is that it—all these years—have you borne grief for him—Ragnfrid?”
> She trembled violently but said nothing.
> “Ragnfrid?” he asked as before. “But then, after Bjørgulf was dead—and later—when you—when you wanted—I should be toward you—as I could not be. Were you thinking of the other then?” he whispered, frightened, bewildered, and tormented.
> “How can you conceive such thoughts?” she whispered through tears.
> Lavrans placed his forehead against his wife’s and shook his head a little.
> “I do not know. You are so strange—everything you said tonight. I grew afraid, Ragnfrid. I suppose I do not understand a woman’s mind—”
> Ragnfrid smiled faintly and put her hands around his neck:
> “God knows, Lavrans—I begged you because I loved you more than is good for a human soul. And I hated the other so that I felt the devil rejoiced in it.”
> “I have loved you, my wife,” said Lavrans softly and kissed her, “with all my heart. You know that? It seemed to me that we had lived well together—Ragnfrid?”
> “You were the best husband,” she said with a little sob and hid herself against him.
> He seized her ardently:
> “Tonight I should like to sleep with you, Ragnfrid. And if you would be toward me as in the old days, then I would not be—such a fool—”
> His wife stiffened in his arms and drew a little away:
> “It is a fast now,” she said softly—strangely hard.
> “So it is.” The man laughed a little. “You and I, Ragnfrid, have kept every fast and tried to live according to God’s commandments in all things. And now it almost seems to me—we might have been happier if we had had more to repent of.”
> “Do not speak so,” his wife begged desperately, pressing her thin hands around his temples. “You surely know that I do not wish you to do anything but what you yourself think right.”
> He drew her to him once more—he groaned aloud as he did so:
> “God help her. God help us all, my Ragnfrid.
> “—I am tired,” he said and let her go. “You are going to rest now too?”
> He stood by the door and waited while she extinguished the hearth, blew out the small iron lamp by the loom, and pinched out the spark. Together they went through the rain toward the dwelling house.
> Lavrans already had his foot on the loft stairs when he turned back to his wife, who was still standing in the vestibule doorway. He drew her ardently to him one last time and kissed her in the darkness. Then he made the sign of the cross over his wife’s face and went upstairs.
> Ragnfrid threw off her clothes and crept into bed. For a while she lay listening to her husband’s steps above in the upper loft-room—then the bed creaked overhead and all grew still. Ragnfrid clasped her slender arms over her withered breasts.
> Yes, God help me. What kind of woman was she, then; what kind of mother was she? She was soon old. And still she was the same. She no longer begged as when they were young, when she had stormed and implored this man who closed himself off, shy and bashful, whenever she grew ardent—grew cold when she wished to give him more than his husband’s right. So it had been—and so she had come to be with child, time after time—humiliated, raging with shame because she could not content herself with his lukewarm husbandly love. Then, when she went about needing kindness and tenderness, he had had so much to give—the man’s tireless, gentle solicitude for her when she was ill and troubled fell like dew over her heated mind. He gladly took up and carried all that was hers—but there was something of his own he would not give. She had loved her children so that it was as though her heart were torn out of her whenever she lost one—God, God, what was she, then, who in the midst of her torments had been able to taste this drop of sweetness, that he took her grief into himself and laid it close against his own?
> Kristin—she would gladly have walked through fire for her daughter—they did not believe it, neither Lavrans nor the child, but it was so. Yet she felt an anger resembling hatred toward her now—it was to forget his sorrow over the child’s sorrow that tonight he had wished he could surrender himself to his wife.
> —Ragnfrid dared not rise, for she did not know whether Kristin might be lying awake in the other bed. But she raised herself soundlessly to her knees, and with her forehead bowed against the footboard, she tried to pray. For her daughter, for her husband, and for herself. While the cold little by little stiffened her body, she set out once again on one of her familiar nightly wanderings, trying to break a path to a peaceful home for her heart.
> 111.
> Haugen lay high up the slope on the west side of the valley. On this moonlit night the whole world was white. Wave after wave of white mountain arched beneath the pale blue, star-poor sky. Even the shadows cast by peaks and ridges across the snowfields seemed strangely light and bright, for the moon sailed so high.
> Down toward the valley, the forest stood loosely white with snow and hoarfrost around the white expanses of meadows, with their little tracery of fences and houses. But at the very bottom of the valley, the shadows thickened into darkness.
> Lady Aashild came out of the cowshed, closed the door behind her, and stood for a while in the snow. The whole world white, and still there were more than three weeks before Advent began. Saint Clement’s Mass cold—then winter had surely come in earnest. Ah yes, that usually went with a year of famine—.
> The old woman sighed heavily into the desolation. Winter again, and cold, and loneliness.—Then she picked up the milk pail and lantern and walked toward the dwelling house. She looked out once more.
> Four black dots came out of the forest halfway down the slope. Four mounted men—the point of a spear flashed in the moonlight. Their progress uphill was heavy—no one had come this way since the snowfall. Could they be coming here—?
> Four armed men—. No one with lawful business with her would be likely to travel in such company. She thought of the chest containing her property and Bjørn’s. Should she hide in the outbuilding?
> She looked out over the winter and the desolation around her. Then she went into the room. The two old dogs that had lain before the smoke-oven thumped their tails against the floorboards. Bjørn had the young dogs with him in the mountains.
> She blew on the embers in the oven and put in wood, filled the iron pot with snow, and set it by the fire. She poured the milk into a wooden dish and carried it into the chamber behind the room.
> Aashild changed out of the dirty, undyed homespun gown that stank of cowshed and sweat, put on a dark-blue garment, and exchanged her coarse linen kerchief for a white linen cloth, which she arranged around her head and neck. She took off her fur-lined boots and put on shoes with silver buckles.
> Then she set about making her room ready—smoothed the cushions and hides in the bed where Bjørn had lain during the day, wiped the long table, and straightened the bench cushions.
> Lady Aashild stood before the oven, stirring the evening porridge, when the dogs gave warning. She heard horses in the courtyard, men came into the gallery, and someone struck the door with a spear. Aashild lifted the pot from the fire, straightened her clothing, and, with the dogs beside her, went forward and opened the door.
> Outside in the moonlit courtyard three young men held four frost-covered horses. The man standing in the gallery called out joyfully:
> “Aunt Aashild, do you come yourself to open the door! Then I must say, Ben trouvé!”
> “Nephew—why, is it you! Then I say the same to you! Go into the room while I show your men to the stable.”
> “Are you alone on the farm?” asked Erlend. He went along while she showed the men where to go.
> “Yes, Sir Bjørn and our farmhand went into the mountains with the sledge—they were to see about bringing home some fodder we have stored by the mountain shelter,” said Lady Aashild. “And I have no serving woman,” she said, laughing.
> A little later the four young men sat on the outer bench with their backs to the table, watching the old lady as she quietly and busily set food before them.
> She laid a cloth on the table and placed a lighted candle upon it, brought butter, cheese, a bear ham, and a tall stack of thin, fine flatbreads. She fetched ale and mead from the cellar beneath the room, and then she ladled the porridge into a fine wooden dish and bade them sit down and begin.
> “It is little for you young fellows,” she said, laughing. “I shall have to cook another pot of porridge. Tomorrow you shall fare better—but I close the cookhouse in winter except when I bake or brew. We are few people here on the farm, and I am beginning to grow old, kinsman.”
> Erlend laughed and shook his head. He noticed that his retainers behaved toward the old woman with such courtesy and reverence as he had never seen them display before.
> “You are a strange woman, Aunt. Mother was ten years younger than you are, and she looked older when last we were with you than you do tonight.”
> “Yes, youth left Magnhild quickly enough,” said Lady Aashild softly. “Where have you come from now?” she asked a little later.
> “I have been staying for a while at a farm north in Lesja,” said Erlend. “I had hired lodging there. I do not know whether you can guess what business I have in these districts?”
> “You mean whether I know that you have had a suit made for Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn’s daughter down here at Jørundgaard?” said Lady Aashild.
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “I had her asked for in seemly and honorable fashion, and Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn answered flatly no. Now I know no better course, since Kristin and I will not allow ourselves to be forced apart, than to carry her away by force. I have—I have had a spy here in the district, and I know that her mother was to be at Sundbu from Saint Clement’s Mass onward for a time, while Lavrans is out at the headland with the other men who are to fetch the winter goods to Sil.”
> Lady Aashild sat for a while:
> “That course, Erlend, you would do better to abandon,” she said. “Nor do I believe the maiden will follow you willingly, and surely you will not use force.”
> “Yes, she will. We have spoken of this many times—she herself has asked me many times to carry her away.”
> “Has Kristin—!” said Lady Aashild. Then she laughed. “For that very reason you should not rely upon getting the maiden to go with you when you come to take her at her word.”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “And now I had thought, Aunt, that you might send word to Jørundgaard asking whether Kristin might come and visit you—for a week or so, while her parents are away. Then we could be at Hamar before anyone notices she is gone,” he explained.
> Lady Aashild answered, still smiling slightly:
> “Have you also thought what Sir Bjørn and I are to answer when Lavrans comes and demands an accounting from us for his daughter?”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. “There were four armed men, and the maiden was willing.”
> “I will not help you with this,” said the lady vehemently. “Lavrans has been faithful to us for many years; he and his wife are honorable people, and I will not lend myself to betraying them or dishonoring her. Leave that maiden in peace, Erlend. It is high time too that your kinsmen heard of some deed from you other than your roaming in and out of the country with stolen women—”
> “We must speak privately, Aunt,” said Erlend shortly.
> Lady Aashild took a candle, went into the chamber, and closed the door behind her. She sat down on a flour chest; Erlend stood with his hands thrust into his belt, looking down at her.
> “You can also tell Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn that Sira Jon at Gerdarud married us before we traveled on to Lady Ingebjørg Haakonsdatter in Sweden.”
> “So,” said Lady Aashild. “Do you know whether Lady Ingebjørg will receive you well when you arrive there?”
> “I spoke with her in Tunsberg,” said Erlend. “She greeted me as her dear kinsman and thanked me for offering her my service here or in Sweden. And Munan has promised to give me letters for her.”
> “Then you surely know,” said Lady Aashild, “that even if you can find a priest to marry you, Kristin will have forfeited all right to property and inheritance from her father. And her children will not be your lawful heirs. It is uncertain whether she will be regarded as your wife.”
> “Perhaps not in this country. That is also why I seek refuge in Sweden. Her forefather, Laurentius the Lawman, was never married to Maiden Bengta in any other way—they never obtained her brother’s consent. And yet she was regarded as a lady—”
> “There were no children,” said Lady Aashild. “Do you think my sons will keep their hands from your inheritance if Kristin is left a widow with children and it can be disputed whether they were born in wedlock!”
> “You wrong Munan,” said Erlend. “I know little of your other children—you have no reason to feel kindly toward them, I know. But Munan has always been fully faithful to me as a kinsman—he greatly wishes me to marry; he sued for me with Lavrans.—Otherwise I can adopt into inheritance and lineage the children we have.”
> “By doing so you brand the mother as a concubine,” said Lady Aashild. “But I do not understand how that meek man, Jon Helgesøn, will dare bring himself into conflict with his bishop by marrying you against the law.”
> “I confessed to him this summer,” said Erlend quietly. “Then he promised to marry us if every other course should fail.”
> “So,” said Lady Aashild. “Then you have loaded a grievous sin upon yourself, Erlend. Kristin had a good home with her father and mother—a good marriage with a handsome and honorable man of good lineage had been arranged for her.”
> “Kristin herself has said,” Erlend replied, “that you spoke of how she and I might suit each other well. And that Simon Andressøn was no husband for her.”
> “Oh, I have said and I have said,” his aunt snapped. “I have said so much in my time.—Nor do I understand how you could have gained your will with Kristin so easily. You cannot have met many times. And I would not have believed that maiden easy to win—.”
> “We met in Oslo,” said Erlend. “Later she was with her uncle at Gerdarud. She came out and met me in the forest.” He looked down and said very softly: “I had her there alone to myself—.”
> Lady Aashild sprang up. Erlend bowed his head still lower.
> “And after that she was—was friendly with you?” asked the lady incredulously.
> “Yes.” Erlend smiled weakly and tremulously. “We were friends after that. And it was not so greatly against her will—but she is without guilt. It was then that she wanted me to carry her away—she did not wish to return to her kinsmen—”
> “But you would not?”
> “No, I wished to try to make her my wife with her father’s consent.”
> “Was this long ago?” asked Lady Aashild.
> “It was a year ago around Saint Lawrence’s Mass,” Erlend replied.
> “You have not been hasty in making your suit,” said the other.
> “She had not been released from the former betrothal,” Erlend replied.
> “And since then you have not trespassed against her?” asked Aashild.
> “We managed matters so that we met a few times”—again the trembling smile passed over the man’s face—“at a house in the town.”
> “In God’s name,” said Lady Aashild. “I shall help you and her as much as I can. I understand it must be too hard for Kristin to remain there with her parents while bearing such a thing. There is no more, is there?” she asked.
> “Not that I have heard of,” said Erlend shortly.
> “Have you thought,” asked the lady after a while, “that Kristin has friends and kinsmen down through the valley?”
> “We must travel in secrecy as much as we can,” said Erlend. “That is why we must set off quickly, so that we are some distance away before her father comes home. You must lend us your sledge, Aunt.”
> 18—Undset: Kristin.
> Aashild shrugged:
> “Then there is her uncle at Skog—what if he hears that you are celebrating a wedding with his brother’s daughter at Gerdarud?”
> “Aasmund has spoken for me to Lavrans,” said Erlend. “He cannot be privy to it, that is true, but he will surely close one eye—we must reach the priest by night and travel onward by night. Then I think Aasmund will afterward point out to Lavrans that it does not become a God-fearing man such as he to separate us once we have been married by a priest—he must rather give his consent so that we become lawful spouses. You must say the same to the man. He may set whatever terms he wishes for reconciliation between us and demand such compensation as he thinks reasonable.”
> “I do not think Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn will be easy to counsel in this matter,” said Lady Aashild. “God and Saint Olav know that I dislike this undertaking, nephew. But I understand that it is the last course you can take if you are to repair the harm you have done Kristin. I shall ride to Jørundgaard myself tomorrow, if you will lend me one of your retainers and I can get Ingrid north of the hill to tend my livestock.”
> Lady Aashild came to Jørundgaard the next evening just as the moonlight mingled with the last gleam of daylight. She saw how pale and thin-cheeked Kristin had become when the young woman came into the courtyard to receive the guest.
> The lady sat by the oven and played with the two little ones. Secretly she watched Kristin searchingly as she went about setting the table. Thin and quiet she looked. She had always been quiet, but there was a different kind of stillness about the girl now. Lady Aashild guessed at all the tension and stubborn defiance that lay behind it.
> “You have surely heard,” said Kristin, coming over to her, “about what happened here this autumn?”
> “Yes, that my nephew has sued for you?”
> “Do you remember,” asked Kristin, “that you once said he and I might suit each other well? Except that he was much too rich and highborn for me?”
> “I hear that Lavrans is of another opinion,” said the lady dryly.
> A flash came into Kristin’s eyes, and she smiled a little. She is good enough for him, Lady Aashild thought. Much as she disliked it, she must yield to Erlend and give him the assistance he had asked for.
> Kristin made up the parents’ bed for the guest, and Lady Aashild asked the young woman to sleep with her. After they had lain down and the room had grown quiet, Lady Aashild stated her errand.
> Her heart grew strangely heavy at seeing that this child seemed not to think at all of the sorrow she would cause her parents. Yet I had lived in grief and torment with Baard for more than twenty years, thought the lady. But perhaps it is so for us all. Kristin did not even seem to have noticed how Ulvhild had declined that autumn—Aashild thought it unlikely that she would see her little sister alive again. But she said nothing of it—the longer Kristin could sustain this wild joy and recklessness, the better it might be.
> Kristin rose, and in the darkness she gathered her jewelry into a small casket, which she brought back to the bed. Then Lady Aashild nevertheless said:
> “Even so it seems to me, Kristin, that it would be a better course for Erlend to ride down here when your father comes home—that he should openly say he has done you a great wrong—and place his case in Lavrans’s hands.”
> “Then I think Father would kill Erlend,” said Kristin.
> “Lavrans will not do that if Erlend refuses to draw his sword against his father-in-law,” the lady replied.
> “I do not want Erlend to be humiliated like that,” said Kristin. “And I do not want Father to know that Erlend had touched me before he asked for me with honor and dignity.”
> “Do you think Lavrans will be less angry,” asked Aashild, “when he learns that you have fled the farm with him, and do you think that will be easier for him to bear? By law you will be nothing but Erlend’s concubine as long as you live with him without your father having given you away.”
> “That is another matter,” said Kristin, “if I become Erlend’s concubine when he could not have me as his lawful wife.”
> Lady Aashild was silent. She thought of how she would have to face Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn when he came home and learned that his daughter had been stolen away.
> Then Kristin said:
> “I understand, Lady Aashild, that you think me an evil child. But since Father came home from the assembly, things here on the farm have been such that every day has been a torment for him as well as for me. It is best for everyone that this matter now reach an end.”
> They rode from Jørundgaard in good time the next day and reached Haugen shortly after noon. Erlend met them in the courtyard, and Kristin threw herself into his arms without caring about the retainer who had accompanied Lady Aashild and her.
> Inside the room she greeted Bjørn Gunnarsøn and then Erlend’s two retainers as though she had known them well before. Lady Aashild could discern no sign that she was either shy or afraid. And later, when they sat at table and Erlend laid out his plan, Kristin joined in the counsel and advised concerning the route: they should ride from Haugen the next evening so late that they reached Rosten when the moon went down, and travel in darkness through Sil until they had passed Loptsgaard; thence up along the Otta to the bridge and on the west side of the Otta and the Laagen by deserted roads as far as the horses could endure. They could rest through the day at one of the spring shielings on the slope, “for as far as Holledi’s judicial district extends, we may encounter people who know me.”
> “Have you thought of fodder for the horses?” said Lady Aashild. “You cannot steal fodder from people’s spring shielings in a year like this—if there is fodder there—and you know no one has fodder to sell in the valley this year.”
> “I have thought of it,” Kristin replied. “You must lend us fodder and food for three days. That is also a reason why we should not travel in so large a band—Erlend must send Jon back to Husaby. The year has been better in Trøndelag, and some loads can surely be brought across the mountain before Christmas. There are some poor people south in the district whom I should like you to help with alms from Erlend and me, Lady Aashild.”
> Bjørn burst into a strange, joyless roar of laughter. Lady Aashild shook her head. But the retainer Ulv raised his swarthy, sharp face and looked at Kristin with a peculiar insolent smile:
> “There is never abundance at Husaby, Kristin Lavransdatter, either in good years or in famine years. But perhaps it will be different when you take command there. From your speech, you sound like the mistress Erlend needs.”
> Kristin nodded calmly to the man and continued. They must keep as far as possible away from the public road. And it seemed impossible to her to take the route through Hamar.
> Erlend objected that Munan was there—it was a matter of this letter to the duchess.
> “Then Ulv must leave us at Fagaberg and ride to Sir Munan, while we keep west of Mjøsa and ride through Land and by the back roads over Hadeland down to Hakedal. From there, I have heard my uncle speak of a deserted road running south to Margretadal. It is impossible for us to ride through Raumarike at the time when the great wedding is to be held at Dyfrin,” she said, laughing.
> Erlend went over and put his arm around her shoulder, and she leaned back against him without caring about all the people who sat watching. Lady Aashild said indignantly:
> “No one would think otherwise than that you had run away from home before.”
> And Sir Bjørn roared with laughter as before.
> A little later Lady Aashild rose to go into the cookhouse and prepare the food. She had lit a fire there, for Erlend’s retainers were to sleep there that night. She bade Kristin come with her, “for I wish to be able to swear to Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn that you were not alone together for any length of time in my house,” she said angrily.
> Kristin laughed and went with the lady. Soon afterward Erlend came sauntering in to them, pulled a three-legged stool up to the hearth, and sat there getting in the women’s way. He seized Kristin whenever she came near him as she flew about her tasks. At last he pulled her down onto his knee.
> “It is surely as Ulv says, that you are the mistress I need.”
> “Indeed,” said Aashild, laughing and annoyed, “you may be well served by her. She is the one who stakes everything on this adventure—you risk little.”
> “That is so,” said Erlend. “But I have shown my willingness to go to her by the proper road. Do not be so angry, Aunt Aashild.”
> “I must surely be angry,” said the lady. “No sooner had you put your affairs in order than you arranged matters so that you must flee from it all with a woman.”
> “You must remember, Aunt,” said Erlend. “It has always been so that it was not the worst men who brought disorder upon themselves for a woman’s sake—all the sagas say so.”
> “Oh, God help us,” said Aashild. Her face grew young and soft. “I have heard that speech before, Erlend”—she took hold of his head and tousled his hair.
> At that moment Ulv Haldorsøn tore open the door and quickly closed it behind him:
> “Another guest has come to the farm, Erlend—the one you would least like to see, I think.”
> “Is it Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn?” said Erlend, springing up.
> “It is not so well,” said the retainer. “It is Eline Ormsdatter.”
> The door was opened from outside; the woman who entered pushed Ulv aside and came forward into the light. Kristin looked over at Erlend. At first it was as though he withered and collapsed; then he straightened, dark red in the face.
> “Where the devil have you come from—what do you want here!”
> Lady Aashild stepped forward and said:
> “You must come up to the main room, Eline Ormsdatter—we still have enough custom here on the farm not to receive our guests in the cookhouse.”
> “I do not expect, Lady Aashild,” said the other, “that Erlend’s kinsmen will greet me as a guest.—You asked where I came from—I come from Husaby, as you may well know. I can greet you from Orm and Margret; they are doing well.”
> Erlend did not reply.
> “When I heard that you had had Gissur Arnfinsøn raise money for you, and that you were going south again,” she continued, “I thought you would probably stop with your kinsmen in Gudbrandsdal this time. I knew you had had inquiries made about the daughter of their neighbor.”
> She looked at Kristin for the first time and met the girl’s eyes. Kristin was very pale, but she looked calmly and searchingly at the other woman.
> Kristin was as calm as stone. She had known it from the moment she heard who had come—this was the thought from which she had continually fled; she had sought to drown it with defiance, restlessness, and impatience; all along she had struggled not to think about whether Erlend had freed himself fully and completely from his former concubine. Now it had overtaken her; it was useless to struggle against it any longer. But she did not plead for herself.
> She saw that Eline Ormsdatter was beautiful. She was no longer young, but she was beautiful, and once she must have been radiantly beautiful. She had pushed back her hood; her head was round as a ball and hard, her cheekbones stood out somewhat—but it was easy to see that she had once been fair nevertheless. Her headdress covered only the back of her head; while she spoke, Eline smoothed the golden, wavy hair at her brow beneath the linen. Kristin had never seen a woman with such large eyes; they were dark brown, round, and hard, but beneath the narrow coal-black brows and long lashes they were wondrously beautiful against the golden hair. Her skin and lips were chapped from riding in the cold, but that could not disfigure her too greatly; she was too beautiful for it. The heavy traveling clothes enveloped her figure, but she bore herself in them as only a woman does who walks in the surest pride in the splendor of her body. She was scarcely as tall as Kristin, but she carried herself so erect that she seemed taller than the slender, delicately limbed young woman.
> “Has she been with you at Husaby all this time?” Kristin asked softly.
> “I have not been at Husaby,” said Erlend shortly, growing red again. “I have been at Hestnæs for most of this summer.”
> “This is the news I wished to bring you, Erlend,” said Eline. “You need no longer impose upon your kinsmen and test their hospitality because I govern your house. I was widowed this autumn.”
> Erlend stood as before.
> “It was not I who asked you to come to Husaby and govern last year,” he said laboriously.
> “I heard that everything there had fallen into neglect,” said Eline. “I had so much old affection for you, Erlend, that I thought I should take your welfare in hand—though God knows you have not dealt well with our children or with me.”
> “For the children I have done what I could,” said Erlend. “And you know very well that it was for their sake that I endured your sitting at Husaby. You can hardly think yourself that you benefited either them or me by it,” he said with a scornful smile. “Gissur surely managed to govern there without your help.”
> “Yes, you have always trusted Gissur so well,” said Eline, laughing softly. “But this is the matter, Erlend—now I am free. If you wish it, you can now keep the promise you once gave me.”
> Erlend was silent.
> “Do you remember,” asked Eline, “the night I bore your son? Then you promised that you would marry me when Sigurd died.”
> Erlend swept his hand beneath his sweat-soaked hair.
> “Yes, I remember it,” he said.
> “Will you keep that word now?” asked Eline.
> “No,” said Erlend.
> Eline Ormsdatter looked over at Kristin—smiled a little and nodded. Then she looked at Erlend again.
> “It is ten years ago, Eline,” said the man. “Since then we have lived together year after year like two damned souls in hell.”
> “Surely not only that!” she said with the same smile.
> “It is years and years since it was anything else,” said Erlend, exhausted. “It cannot help the children. And you know—you know that I can scarcely bear to be in the same room with you anymore,” he almost screamed.
> “I noticed nothing of that when you were home this summer,” said Eline with an eloquent smile. “Then we were not enemies—always.”
> “If you count that as our being friends, then so be it,” said Erlend wearily.
> “Are you to stand here?” said Lady Aashild. She ladled from the pot into two large wooden dishes and gave one to Kristin. The girl took it. “Carry it across—you, Ulv, take the other—put them on the table; we must have supper whether matters stand thus or otherwise.”
> Kristin and the retainer went with the dishes of food. Lady Aashild said to the other two:
> “Come now, both of you; it is useless to stand here barking at each other.”
> “It is better that Eline and I speak this through now,” said Erlend.
> Lady Aashild said no more but went away.
> In the main room Kristin had set the table and fetched ale from the cellar. She sat on the outer bench, straight as a candle and calm of face, but she did not eat. Neither Bjørn nor Erlend’s retainers had much appetite. Only the man who had come with Eline and Bjørn’s farmhand ate. Lady Aashild sat down and ate a little porridge. No one spoke a word.
> At long last Eline Ormsdatter came in alone. Lady Aashild offered her a place between Kristin and herself; Eline sat down and ate something. Now and then the reflected gleam of a hidden smile passed over her face, and she glanced sidelong at Kristin.
> After a while Lady Aashild went out to the cookhouse.
> The fire on the hearth had nearly burned out. Erlend sat on the three-legged stool near it, huddled with his head down in his arms.
> Lady Aashild went over and laid a hand on his shoulder:
> “God forgive you, Erlend, for the way you have arranged this—!”
> Erlend looked up; his face was grimy with misery.
> “She is with child,” he said and closed his eyes.
> Lady Aashild’s face flamed; she gripped his shoulder hard:
> “Which of them?” she asked coarsely and mockingly.
> “Well, it is not mine,” said Erlend, as extinguished as before. “But you will surely not believe me—no one will—” He collapsed in upon himself again.
> Lady Aashild sat down before him on the edge of the hearth.
> “You must try to master yourself now, Erlend. It is not so easy to believe you in this matter. Do you swear that it is not yours?”
> Erlend raised his ravaged face:
> “As surely as I need God’s mercy—as surely as I hope that—that God has comforted Mother with Him for all she had to endure down here—I have not touched Eline since the first time I saw Kristin!” He shouted it, so that Lady Aashild hushed him.
> “Then I do not understand why this is such a misfortune. You must find out who the father is and buy him into marrying her.”
> “I think it is Gissur Arnfinsøn—my steward at Husaby,” said Erlend wearily. “We spoke of it last autumn—and later too—. Sigurd’s death had been expected for some time. He was willing to marry her when she became a widow if I would give her a suitable dowry—”
> “Yes,” said Lady Aashild. Erlend continued:
> “She swears she will not have him. She will name me as the father. If I clear myself by oath—do you think anyone will believe anything but that I swear falsely—”
> “You must be able to dissuade her from this,” said Lady Aashild. “There is no other course now: tomorrow you must go home with her to Husaby. Then you must be hard and firm and arrange this marriage between your steward and Eline.”
> “Yes,” said Erlend. Then he threw himself forward and sobbed aloud.
> “Do you not understand, Aunt—what do you think Kristin will believe—”
> That night Erlend lay in the cookhouse with his retainers. In the room Kristin slept with Lady Aashild in her bed, and Eline Ormsdatter in the other bed there. Bjørn went out and lay down in the stable.
> The next morning Kristin went with Lady Aashild to the cowshed. While the lady went to the cookhouse to prepare the morning meal, Kristin carried the milk up to the room.
> A candle burned on the table. Eline was dressed and sat on the edge of her bed. Kristin greeted her quietly, fetched a strainer, and strained the milk.
> “Will you give me a drink of milk?” asked Eline. Kristin took a wooden ladle and held it out to the other woman; she drank greedily and looked at Kristin over the rim of the cup.
> “So you are that Kristin Lavransdatter who has parted me from Erlend’s friendship,” she said and handed back the ladle.
> “You must know yourself whether there was any friendship to part,” replied the young woman.
> Eline bit her lip.
> “What will you do,” she said, “if Erlend grows tired of you and one day tries to marry you to his serving man? Will you yield to Erlend in that too?”
> Kristin did not answer; then the other woman laughed and said:
> “Now you yield to him in all things, I imagine. What do you say, Kristin—shall we cast dice for our man, we two concubines of Erlend Nikulaussøn?” When she received no answer, she laughed again and said: “Are you so simple that you do not deny being a concubine?”
> “I will not trouble to lie to you,” said Kristin.
> “It would not profit you much either,” replied Eline as before. “I know the boy. He must have strutted before you like a blackcock the second time you were together, I imagine. It is a pity for you too, fair child standing there.”
> Kristin’s cheeks whitened. Sick with loathing, she said softly:
> “I will not speak with you—”
> “Do you think he will deal better with you than with me?” Eline began. Then Kristin replied sharply:
> “I shall not complain of Erlend, whatever he does. I myself went astray—and I shall not whine and lament if the path leads over the scree—”
> Eline was silent for a while. Then she said, red and uncertain:
> “I too was a maiden when he had me, Kristin—although I had been called the old man’s wife for seven years. But perhaps you cannot understand what a wretched life it was.”
> Kristin began to tremble violently. Eline looked at her. Then she took a small horn from her traveling chest, which stood beside her on the bed step. She broke the seal and said softly:
> “You are young and I am old, Kristin. I know it is useless for me to struggle against you—now it is your time. Will you drink with me, Kristin?”
> Kristin did not move. Then the other raised the horn to her mouth. Kristin noticed that she did not drink. Eline said:
> “You can surely show me the honor of drinking to me—and promise that you will not be a harsh stepmother to my children?”
> Kristin took the horn. At that moment Erlend opened the door. He stood for a while, looking from one woman to the other.
> “What is this?” he asked.
> Then Kristin answered, and her voice was shrill and wild:
> “We are drinking to each other, we two concubines of yours—”
> He seized her wrist and took the horn. “Be silent,” he said harshly. “You shall not drink with her.”
> “Why not?” said Kristin as before. “She was as pure as I when you enticed her—”
> “She has said that so often that I think she believes it herself,” said Erlend. “Do you remember when you got me to go to Sigurd with that tale, and he brought witnesses that he had caught you before with another man?”
> White with disgust, Kristin turned away. Eline had grown dark red; then she said defiantly:
> “Still, she will hardly become a leper if she drinks with me.”
> Erlend turned angrily toward Eline—then all at once his face seemed to grow long and rigid; the man gasped with terror:
> “Jesus!” he said almost inaudibly. He seized Eline by the arm.
> “Drink to her, then,” he said hard and trembling. “You drink first; then she shall drink with you.”
> Eline twisted away with a groan. She fled backward into the room; the man followed. “Drink,” he said. He tore the dagger from his belt and followed with it in his hand. “Drink the drink you poured for Kristin.” He seized Eline by the arm, dragged her to the table, and forced her forward toward the horn.
> Eline screamed once and hid her face in her arm. Erlend let her go and stood trembling.
> “I had a hell with Sigurd,” Eline screamed. “You—you promised—but you have been worse toward me, Erlend!”
> Then Kristin stepped forward and seized the horn:
> “One of us two must drink—you cannot keep us both—”
> Erlend took the horn from her and flung her across the floor so that she tumbled down by Lady Aashild’s bed. He pressed the drink threateningly toward Eline Ormsdatter’s mouth—stood with one knee on the bench beside her and, with his hand around her head, tried to force the drink into her.
> She reached beneath his arm, snatched the dagger from the table, and struck at the man. The thrust did little more than graze his clothing. Then she turned the point against herself, and a moment later she sagged sideways into his lap.
> Kristin rose and came over. Erlend held Eline; her head hung backward over his arm. She began to rattle almost at once—there was blood in her throat, and it ran from her mouth. She spat out a great deal and said:
> “For you I had intended—that drink—for all the times—you have betrayed me—”
> “Bring Aunt Aashild here,” said Erlend in a low voice. Kristin stood motionless.
