> “She is dying,” said Erlend as before.
> “Then she fares better than we,” said Kristin. Erlend looked at her—the despair in his eyes softened her. She went out of the room.
> “What is it?” asked Lady Aashild when Kristin called her from the cookhouse.
> “We have killed Eline Ormsdatter,” said Kristin. “She is dying—”
> Lady Aashild began to run. But Eline breathed her last as the lady stepped through the door.
> Lady Aashild had laid the dead woman properly upon the bench, washed the blood from her face, and covered it with her headcloth. Erlend stood leaning against the wall behind the corpse.
> “Do you understand,” said Lady Aashild, “that this was the worst thing that could happen?”
> She had filled the oven with wood and twigs; now she placed the horn in the midst of it and blew the fire into flame.
> “Can you rely upon your retainers?” the lady asked again. “I mean Ulv and Haftor.—Jon and the man who came with Eline I know little.”
> “You understand,” said the lady, “if it becomes known that you and Kristin were together here, and that you were alone with her when she died, you might as well have let Kristin drink Eline’s brew.—And if there is talk of poison, people will remember what I have been accused of before.—Did she have any kinsmen or friends?”
> “No,” said Erlend quietly. “She had no one but me.”
> “Even so,” Lady Aashild began, “it may be difficult to conceal this and carry away the corpse without the foulest suspicion falling upon you.”
> “She shall lie in consecrated ground,” said Erlend, “even if it costs me Husaby. What do you say, Kristin?”
> Kristin nodded.
> Lady Aashild sat silent. The more she thought, the more impossible it seemed to find a way out. Four retainers sat in the cookhouse—if Erlend could buy them all into silence, if one of them, if Eline’s retainer, could be bought into leaving the country, it would still remain uncertain. And at Jørundgaard they knew Kristin had been here—if Lavrans learned this, then she could not imagine what he would do. Then there was the matter of removing the dead woman. The mountain road westward was unthinkable now—there remained the road to Raumsdal or over the mountain to Trondheim, or southward through the valley. And if the truth came out, it would never be believed—even if it were accepted as true.
> “I must take counsel with Bjørn about this,” she said, rose, and went out.
> Bjørn Gunnarsøn listened to his wife’s account without moving a muscle and without shifting his eyes from Erlend.
> “Bjørn,” said Aashild desperately. “Someone must swear that he saw her lay hands upon herself.”
> Life slowly darkened in Bjørn’s eyes; he looked at his wife, and his mouth drew into a crooked smile:
> “You mean that this someone should be I?”
> Lady Aashild clasped her hands and raised them toward him. “Bjørn, you understand what is at stake for these two—”
> “And so you think it is over with me anyway?” he asked slowly. “Or do you think enough remains of the man I once was that I dare swear a false oath to save that boy from ruin?
> “I who was myself dragged under—all those years ago. Dragged under, I said,” he insisted.
> “You say that because I am old now,” whispered Aashild.
> Kristin broke into tears so piercing that they cut through the room. She had been sitting in the corner by Aashild’s bed, rigid and silent. Now she began to weep aloud. It was as though Lady Aashild’s voice had torn her heart open. It had been heavy with memories of the sweetness of love; it was as though that voice first made Kristin grasp fully what her and Erlend’s love had been. The memory of hot and ardent happiness washed over everything else—washed away the hard, despairing hatred of this last night. She knew only of her love and her will to endure.
> All three looked at her. Then Sir Bjørn went over, took her beneath the chin, and looked down at her:
> “Do you say, Kristin, that she did it herself?”
> “Every word you have heard is true,” said Kristin firmly. “We threatened her until she did this.”
> “She had intended a worse fate for Kristin,” said Aashild.
> Sir Bjørn let go of the girl. He went over to the corpse, lifted it into the bed where Eline had lain the previous night, laid it against the wall, and drew the covers well over it.
> “You must send Jon and the retainer you do not know home to Husaby with word that Eline is accompanying you south. Let them ride at midday. Say that the women are sleeping in here; they may eat in the cookhouse. Afterward you must speak with Ulv and Haftor. Has she threatened to do this before? So that you can produce witnesses to it if questions are asked?”
> “Every person who was on the farm during the last years we lived together there,” said Erlend wearily, “can testify that she threatened to kill herself—and me too, sometimes—whenever I spoke of wishing to part from her.”
> Bjørn laughed harshly:
> “I thought so. Tonight we shall dress her in her traveling clothes and seat her in the sledge. You must sit beside her—”
> Erlend swayed where he stood:
> “I cannot.”
> “God knows how much man will be left in you when you have had your own way for another twenty years,” said Bjørn. “Do you think you can drive the sledge, then? I shall sit beside her. We must travel by night and on deserted roads until we are down in Fron. In this cold no one can know how long she has been dead. We shall drive to the monks’ hospice at Roaldstad. There you and I shall testify that you quarreled in the back of the sledge. It is attested that you have not wished to live with her since you were released from the ban, and that you have sued for a maiden who is your equal. Ulv and Haftor must keep away from us the whole road, so that if need be they can swear she was alive the last time they saw her. You can surely induce them to do that? At the monks’ you can have her laid in a coffin—and then you must bargain with the priests for burial peace for her and peace for your own soul.
> “—Yes, it is not beautiful. But you have not arranged matters so that it can be more beautiful. Do not stand there like a woman in childbed about to swoon. God help you, boy; surely you have never before felt the edge at the knot of your throat.”
> A biting wind came down from the mountain—fine, silvery snow smoked from the drifts into the moon-blue air when the men were to depart.
> Two horses had been harnessed one before the other. Erlend sat at the front of the sledge. Kristin went over to him:
> “This time, Erlend, you must try to send me word of how the journey goes and what becomes of you.”
> He squeezed her hand so hard that she thought the blood must burst from the roots of her nails.
> “Do you dare hold fast to me still, Kristin?”
> “Yes, still,” she said, and after a little while: “In this deed we both bear guilt—I incited you, for I wished her dead.”
> Lady Aashild and Kristin stood watching the travelers. The sledge dipped up and down over the drifts. It vanished into a hollow—came into view farther down on a white field. Then the men passed into the shadow of a ridge and disappeared for good.
> The two women sat before the oven with their backs to the empty bed, from which Lady Aashild had carried the clothing and straw. Both felt that it stood there empty and gaping behind them.
> “Would you like us to sleep in the cookhouse tonight?” Lady Aashild said once.
> “It makes no difference where we lie down,” said Kristin.
> Lady Aashild went out and looked at the weather.
> “Yes, if the wind rises or a thaw comes, they will not get far before it becomes known,” said Kristin.
> “It always blows here at Haugen,” replied Lady Aashild. “There is no sign of a change in the weather.”
> Then they sat as before.
> “You must not forget,” said the lady once, “what fate she had intended for you.”
> Kristin replied softly:
> “I think perhaps I would have wished the same in her place.”
> “You would never have wished to cause another person to become a leper,” said Lady Aashild vehemently.
> “Do you remember, Aunt, that you once told me it is good when a person does not dare do what she does not think beautiful? But it is not so good when she thinks a thing is not beautiful because she does not dare do it.”
> “You would not dare do it because of the sin,” said Lady Aashild.
> “No, I do not think so,” said Kristin. “It is so that I have already done much that I once would have thought I dared not do because of the sin. But I did not see then that this comes with sin: one must trample others down.”
> “Erlend wished to end his dissolute life long before he met you,” Aashild replied vehemently. “It was over between the two of them before that.”
> “I know,” said Kristin. “But she can never have had reason to believe Erlend’s resolutions were so firm that she could not shake them.”
> “Kristin,” Lady Aashild begged anxiously, “surely you will not let go of Erlend now. Now you cannot be saved unless you save one another.”
> “A priest would hardly counsel thus,” said Kristin with a cold smile. “But I know I shall not let go of Erlend—not even if I must trample down my own father.”
> Lady Aashild rose.
> “We may as well find something to do as sit like this,” she said. “It would surely be futile to try to sleep.”
> She fetched the butter churn from the chamber, carried in several pans of milk, filled it, and stood ready to churn.
> “Let me do this,” Kristin begged. “I have the younger back.”
> They worked without speaking; Kristin stood by the chamber door churning, and Aashild carded wool near the hearth. Only when Kristin had strained the churn and was kneading the butter did the young woman suddenly ask:
> “Aunt Aashild—are you never afraid of the day when you must stand before God’s judgment?”
> Lady Aashild rose, came over, and stood before Kristin in the light:
> “Perhaps I shall have the courage to ask Him who created me as I am whether He will have mercy on me when His time comes. For I never asked for His mercy when I acted against His commandment. And I have never asked God or man to remit one penny of the penance I have paid here in this world.”
> A little later she said softly:
> “Munan, my eldest son, was twenty years old. He was not then as I know he is now. They were not like that then, my children—”
> Kristin replied quietly:
> “Yet you have had Sir Bjørn at your side every day and every night through all these years.”
> “Yes—I have had that too,” said Aashild.
> A little later Kristin finished making the butter. Lady Aashild then said they should try to lie down for a while. In the dark bed she put an arm around Kristin’s shoulder and drew the young head to her. It was not long before she heard from Kristin’s even, quiet breathing that she had fallen asleep.
> IV.
> The frost persisted. In every cowshed in the district the half-starved animals lowed and complained and suffered from the cold. But already the people were saving fodder as much as they possibly could.
> There was not much visiting at Christmas that year; people remained at home, each in his own place.
> At Christmas the cold increased—it was as though each day seemed colder than the one before. People could scarcely remember so hard a winter—no more snow fell either, not even in the mountains, but the snow that had come around Saint Clement’s Mass froze hard as stone. The sun shone from a clear sky now that the days were beginning to lengthen. At night the northern lights crackled and flickered over the mountain ridges in the north; they flickered across half the sky—but they brought no change in the weather. Now and then a day would cloud over, a little dry snow would sift down, and then there would be clear weather and bitter cold again. The Laagen murmured and clucked dully beneath its bridges of ice.
> Every morning Kristin thought that now she could bear no more, that she would not endure to the end of that day. For she felt each day to be like a single combat between her father and herself. And they had to stand thus against each other now, when every living thing, people and livestock, throughout the districts suffered under a trial. But when evening came, she had endured.
> It was not that her father was unfriendly. They never spoke of what lay between them, but behind everything he did not say she felt that he was inflexibly determined to persist in his refusal.
> And she burned with longing for his friendship. It burned so terribly because she knew how much else her father had to bear—and had things been as before, he would have spoken with her about it.—It was true that they were better provided for at Jørundgaard than in most other places, but here too they felt the famine every day and every hour. In other winters Lavrans had used to break and train his young colts, but that autumn he had sold them all in the south of the country. His daughter missed hearing his voice out in the courtyard and seeing him grapple with the slim, shaggy two-year-olds in the play he loved so much. The farm’s storehouses, barns, and bins had not been entirely empty after the previous harvest, but many people also came to Jørundgaard asking for help, whether to buy or as a gift, and no one asked in vain.
> Late one evening a huge old man clothed in skins came on skis. Lavrans spoke with him outside in the courtyard, and Halvdan carried food to him in the hearth-room. No one on the farm who had seen him knew who he was—likely he was one of the people who lived in the mountains; perhaps Lavrans had encountered him there. But her father did not mention the visit, nor did Halvdan.
> But one evening there came a man with whom Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn had had some unsettled affair for many years. Lavrans went to the storehouse with him. But when he came back into the room, he said:
> “Everyone wants help from me. But here on my farm you are all against me. You too, wife,” he said vehemently to Ragnfrid.
> Then her mother sprang at Kristin.
> “Do you hear what your father says to me! I am not against you, Lavrans. You know too, Kristin, what happened down here at Roaldstad late in the autumn, when he traveled down the valley with that other adulterer, his kinsman from Haugen—she took her own life, the unhappy woman whom he had enticed away from all her kinsmen.”
> Rigid and hard, Kristin replied:
> “I understand that you blame him as much for the years in which he struggled to leave his sin as for the years in which he lived in it.”
> “Jesus, Mary,” cried Ragnfrid, striking her hands together. “What have you become! Could not even this cause you to change your mind!”
> “No,” said Kristin. “I have not changed my mind.”
> Then Lavrans looked up from the bench where he sat beside Ulvhild:
> “Nor have I, Kristin,” he said quietly.
> But Kristin knew in her heart that in a way she had changed, if not her mind, then her view. She had received word of how that disastrous journey had ended. It had gone more easily than might have been expected. Whether because cold had settled in the wound or for some other reason, the knife thrust Erlend had received in the breast had turned bad; he had lain ill for a time in the hospice at Roaldstad. Sir Bjørn had nursed him during those days. But because Erlend was wounded, it had been easier to explain the other matter and be believed.
> When he was able to travel on, he had taken the dead woman with him in a coffin all the way to Oslo. There, through Sira Jon’s intercession, he had apparently obtained a burial place for her in the churchyard of the ruined Church of Saint Nicholas; then he had confessed to the Bishop of Oslo himself, who had laid upon him a pilgrimage to the Holy Blood at Schwerin. Now he had gone out of the country.
> She could not make pilgrimage to any place and find absolution. For her it was to sit here, wait and think, and try to persist in her resistance to her parents. A strange, wintry-cold light fell over all the memories of her meetings with Erlend. She thought of his violence—in love and in sorrow—and it seemed to her that if she had been able to take up all things as abruptly and storm with them at once, perhaps afterward they would have seemed smaller and easier to bear. At times she thought: perhaps Erlend will give me up. She thought she had always had a small fear that if matters grew too difficult for them, he would give up. But she would not give him up unless he himself released her from every oath.
> Thus the winter wore on. And Kristin could no longer deceive herself, but had to see that the hardest trial awaited them all, for Ulvhild did not have long to live. And amid her bitter grief for her sister, she saw with horror that truly her own soul had been led astray and consumed by sin. For with the dying child and her parents’ unspeakable sorrow before her eyes, she still thought this—if Ulvhild dies, how shall I then bear to look at Father and not cast myself down before him, confess everything, and beg him to forgive me and govern me—.
> They were in Lent. People slaughtered what remained of the small herds they had hoped to keep alive, lest the livestock die of themselves. And people grew ill from living on fish and having so little and such wretched flour food. Sira Eirik released the entire district from the prohibition against milk foods. But the people scarcely had a drop of milk.
> Ulvhild lay bedridden. She lay alone in the sisters’ bed, and someone watched over her every night. It happened that her father and Kristin both sat over her. On such a night Lavrans said to his daughter:
> “Do you remember what Brother Edvin said about Ulvhild’s fate? I thought even then that perhaps he meant this. But then I pushed it away from me.”
> During those nights he sometimes spoke of one thing or another from the time when the children were small. Kristin sat white and desperate and understood that behind the words her father was begging her.
> One day Lavrans went out with Kolbein to seek a bear’s den north in the mountain forest. They came home with a she-bear on a sledge, and Lavrans had a living bear cub inside his tunic. It amused Ulvhild a little when he showed it to her. But Ragnfrid said that this was no time to take on such an animal, and what did he intend to do with it now?
> “I shall raise it to tether before the storehouse for my maidens,” said Lavrans, laughing harshly.
> But they could not obtain the rich milk the bear cub needed, and so Lavrans killed it a few days later.
> The sun gained enough strength that now and then water dripped a little from the roofs at midday. The tits clung and climbed along the sunward timber walls, pecking so that it rang as they sought flies sleeping in the cracks between the logs. Across the fields the snow shone hard and bright as silver.
> At last one evening clouds began to draw across the moon. In the morning the people at Jørundgaard awoke in such a whirl of snow that they could see nothing in any direction.
> That day they understood that Ulvhild was to die.
> The whole household was inside, and Sira Eirik came over. Many candles burned in the room. Early in the evening Ulvhild quietly and peacefully expired in her mother’s arms.
> Ragnfrid bore it better than anyone had expected. The parents sat together, both weeping quite silently. Everyone in the room wept. When Kristin went over to her father, he placed his arm around her shoulder. He felt how she shook and trembled, and then he drew her to him. But it seemed to her that he must feel as though she were farther removed from him than the little dead girl in the bed.
> She did not understand how she endured. She scarcely remembered herself what she endured for, but dull and mute with pain, she remained standing and did not fall at his feet.
> —Then a pair of planks were broken up in the church floor before Saint Thomas’s altar, and a grave was hewn for Ulvhild Lavransdatter in the stone-hard earth beneath. Snow fell thickly and quietly throughout the days the child lay upon the bier; it snowed when she was borne to the earth, and it continued to snow, almost without pause, for an entire month thereafter.
> To the people who went about awaiting the deliverance of spring, it seemed it would never come. The days grew long and bright, and the valley lay in a vapor of melting snow while the sun shone. But the cold remained in the air, and the warmth gained no power. At night it froze hard—the ice cracked, there were booming sounds from within the mountains, and wolves howled and foxes barked down in the district as at midwinter. People stripped bark for the cattle, but the beasts fell dead in great numbers in their stalls. No one understood how this was to end.
> Kristin went out on such a day, when water trickled in the wheel ruts and the snow glittered like silver across the fields. Facing the sun, the snowdrifts were already hollowed out, so that the fine ice lattice along the crust broke with a soft silvery sound when she struck it with her foot. But everywhere, in the smallest shadow, the sharp cold remained in the air and the snow was hard.
> She walked up toward the church—she did not know herself what she wanted there, but she was drawn to it. Her father was there—some farmers, guild brothers, were holding a meeting in the gallery, she knew.
> On the slope she met the band of farmers coming down. Sira Eirik was with them. The men were all on foot; they went in a dark, shaggy cluster, with bowed heads, not speaking together; they returned her greeting curtly as she passed.
> Kristin thought it was long since the time when every person in the district had been her friend. Now they all surely knew that she was an evil daughter. Perhaps they knew more about her too. Now they probably all believed there had been truth in the old talk about her and Arne and Bentein. Perhaps she was in the foulest repute. She raised her head and continued to the church.
> The door stood ajar. It was cold inside the church, but still a kind of warmth flowed toward her mind from this dim, brown space, with its high-striving pillar trunks lifting the darkness toward the roof’s framework. No lights burned upon the altars, but a little sunlight fell through the crack of the door and gleamed faintly upon images and vessels.
> Before Saint Thomas’s altar she saw her father kneeling with his head bowed toward his folded hands, which pressed his cap to his breast.
> Shyly and sorrowfully Kristin slipped out and stood in the gallery. Framed by the arch between the two small pillars she held, she saw Jørundgaard lying below, and behind her home the pale-blue haze over the valley. In the sun the river flashed white with water and ice through the district. But the alder scrub along its course was golden brown with catkins, the spruce forest was spring-green after all up here by the church, and the birds in the grove nearby chirped, twittered, and whistled. Ah yes, there had been such birdsong every evening after sunset.
> And she felt that the longing she had believed must now have been tormented out of her, the longing in her blood and body, stirred now, frail and weak as though awakening from winter sleep.
> Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn came out and locked the church door behind him. He went and stood near his daughter, looking out through the next arch. She saw how the winter had ravaged her father. She did not understand herself how she could touch upon this now, but it burst from her nevertheless.
> “Is it true, as Mother said the other day, that you told her—if it had been Arne Gyrdsøn, you would have yielded to me?”
> “Yes,” said Lavrans, not looking at her.
> “You did not say that while Arne lived,” Kristin replied.
> “It never came up. I understood well enough that the boy loved you—but he said nothing—and he was young—and I never noticed that you thought of him in that way. You could hardly expect me to offer my daughter to a man who owned nothing.” He smiled fleetingly. “But I loved the boy,” he said softly. “And if I had seen you suffering away from love for him—”
> They remained standing and looking out. Kristin felt her father looking at her—she struggled to keep her face calm, but she felt how white she became. Then her father went to her, put both arms around her, and crushed her in his embrace. He bent her head back, looked down into his daughter’s face, and hid it again against his shoulder.
> “Jesus Christ, little Kristin, are you then so unhappy—”
> “I think I shall die of it, Father,” she said against him.
> She burst into tears. But she wept because she had felt in his caress and seen in his eyes that he was now so tormented that he could no longer hold fast to his resistance. She had conquered him.
> In the middle of the night she awoke when her father touched her shoulder in the darkness:
> “Get up,” he said softly. “Do you hear—?”
> Then she heard the singing around the corners of the house—the deep, full note of a moisture-laden south wind. Water streamed from the roof; rain whispered as it fell into soft, thawing snow.
> Kristin threw on her gown and followed her father to the outer door. Together they stood looking out into the bright May night—warm wind and rain struck them; the sky was a welter of tangled, driving rain clouds, the forests roared, the wind whistled between the houses—and from the mountains above they heard the dull thunder of sliding snow.
> Kristin sought her father’s hand and held it. He had called her and wished to show her this. So it had been between them before: he would have done that. And now it was so again.
> When they went back inside to lie down, Lavrans said:
> “The strange retainer who was here during the week carried a letter to me from Sir Munan Baardsøn. He intends to come up here this summer to see his mother, and then he asked whether he might find me and speak with me.”
> “What will you answer him, my father?” she whispered.
> “I cannot tell you now,” Lavrans replied. “But I shall speak with him, and then I must decide as I think I can answer before God, my daughter.”
> Kristin crept back in beside Ramborg, and Lavrans went and lay down beside his sleeping wife. He lay thinking that if the flood came great and sudden, few farms in the district were as exposed as Jørundgaard. There was said to be a prophecy that one day the river would take it.
> V.
> Spring came in one rush. A few days after the thaw began, the district lay black-brown beneath the rainstorms. Water rushed down through the slopes; the river swelled and lay like a lead-gray sea at the valley bottom, with little floating groves upon the surface and a treacherous, bubbling furrow of current. At Jørundgaard the water stood far in over the fields. Yet everywhere the damage proved less than people had feared.
> The spring work came late, and people sowed their scant seed grain with a prayer to God that He might spare it from night frost until autumn. And it appeared that He would now hear them and lighten their burdens a little. June came with changeable weather, the summer was good, and the people turned toward the hope that the traces of the famine year would be erased in time.
> The haymaking was over when, one evening, four men came riding to Jørundgaard. They were two lords with two retainers, Sir Munan Baardsøn and Sir Baard Petersøn of Hestnæs.
> Ragnfrid and Lavrans had the table laid in the upper loft-room and beds prepared for the guests in the storehouse. But Lavrans asked the lords to wait until the next day to state their business, when they had rested after their journey.
> Sir Munan did most of the speaking during the meal; he often addressed Kristin and talked as though they were well acquainted. She saw that her father did not like this. Sir Munan was short, red-faced, ugly, talkative, and somewhat foolish in manner. People called him Munan Stump or Munan Dance. Yet despite this manner of his, Lady Aashild’s son was a sensible and capable man who had served as the Crown’s agent in several matters and surely had some influence among those who governed the realm. He held his mother’s allodial property in Skogheimsherred, was very rich, and had made a rich marriage. Lady Katrin, his wife, was remarkably ugly and seldom opened her mouth, but her husband always spoke of her as though she were the wisest woman, so in jest people called her Lady Katrin the Wise in Counsel and Fair in Speech. They seemed to live well and lovingly together, although Sir Munan was notorious for his wanton conduct both before and after his marriage.
> Sir Baard Petersøn was a handsome and imposing old man, though now somewhat heavy in the belly and limbs. His hair and beard had faded somewhat, but were still as much yellow as white. Since King Magnus Haakonsøn’s death, he had lived quietly and managed his great estates in Nordmøre. He was a widower after his second wife and had many children, all said to be handsome, well brought up, and in good circumstances.
> The next day Lavrans and his guests went to confer in the loft. Lavrans asked his wife to be present, but she would not. “This must lie entirely in your hands. You know it will be the heaviest sorrow for our daughter if this matter does not prosper, but I see well enough that not a little may speak against this marriage.”
> Sir Munan produced a letter from Erlend Nikulaussøn. Erlend offered to let Lavrans determine every condition himself if he would betroth his daughter Kristin to him. Erlend was willing to have his properties valued and his revenues examined by impartial men and to grant Kristin such bridal and morning gifts that she would own one-third of his estate, apart from her own dowry and any inheritance she might receive from her kinsmen, if she should be widowed after him without children who survived their father. Furthermore, he offered to let Kristin govern with full authority her portion of the estate, both what she owned from home and what she received from him. But if Lavrans wished other conditions for the ordering of their property, Erlend would gladly hear him and abide by them. The only condition Kristin’s kinsmen were to bind themselves to in return was that if they became guardians of children born to him and her, they should never attempt to revoke the gifts he had made to his children with Eline Ormsdatter, but should recognize that this property had left his estate before he entered into marriage with Kristin Lavransdatter. Finally, Erlend offered to hold the wedding with all seemly splendor at his own estate of Husaby.
> Lavrans began to speak and said:
> “This is a fair offer. I understand that it lies greatly upon your kinsman’s heart to reach agreement with me. I see that also from the fact that he has induced you, Sir Munan, to travel a second time on such an errand to a man like me, who counts for little outside this district, and that a lord such as you, Sir Baard, has troubled yourself with this journey in support. But now I will say this about Erlend’s offer: my daughter was not reared to govern property and riches herself; I have always intended to give her to a man such that I could safely place the maiden’s welfare in his hands. I do not know whether Kristin is fit to be entrusted with such authority, but I scarcely think she would thrive with it. She is gentle and compliant of disposition—and this is one of the reasons I have borne in mind in opposing this marriage, for Erlend has shown lack of judgment in several matters. Had she been a domineering, bold, and contentious woman, the matter would at once have stood differently.”
> Sir Munan burst into laughter and said:
> “Dear Lavrans, do you complain that the maiden is too little contentious—” and Sir Baard said with a slight smile:
> “Yet your daughter seems to me to have shown that she does not lack will—for two years she has now held fast to Erlend directly against you.”
> Lavrans said:
> “I remember it well enough; nevertheless I know what I am saying. It has been painful for her herself during the time she has defied me, and she will not long be happy with a husband unless he can govern her.”
> “The devil take it,” said Sir Munan. “Then your daughter must be very unlike all the women I have known, for I have never seen one who did not prefer to govern both herself and her husband as well.”
> Lavrans shrugged and did not answer.
> Then Baard Petersøn said:
> “I can imagine, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, that you have acquired no greater liking for this marriage between your daughter and my foster son since matters ended as they did with the woman he had with him. But you must know that it has come to light that this poor woman had allowed herself to be enticed by another man, Erlend’s steward at Husaby. Erlend knew this when he traveled with her down through the valley; he had offered to provide her with a seemly dowry if the man would marry her.”
> “Are you certain this is so?” asked Lavrans. “Even so, I do not know whether the matter becomes fairer from that. It must be hard for a woman of good lineage to enter the estate on the master’s arm, but be led out by the servant.”
> Munan Baardsøn broke in:
> “I understand, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, that most of what you hold against my cousin is that he had this misfortune with Sigurd Saksulvsøn’s wife. And it is true that it was not well. But you must remember, man, in God’s name—there the young lad lived in the house with a young and beautiful wife, and she had an old, cold, incapable husband—and the night is half a year long up there; I do not think much else could have been expected unless Erlend had been a holy man indeed. It cannot be denied that Erlend has never had a monk’s flesh, but I do not think your young, beautiful daughter would thank you if you gave her to a monkish man.—It is true that Erlend behaved foolishly and worse afterward—. But this matter must end sometime—we, his kinsmen, have struggled to help the boy back onto his feet; the woman is dead, and Erlend has done all he could for her body and soul; the Bishop of Oslo himself has absolved him from his sin, and now he has returned home cleansed by the Holy Blood at Schwerin—will you be stricter than the Bishop of Oslo and that archbishop, or whoever it is who governs down there over the precious blood—.
> “Dear Lavrans, it is so that chastity is a beautiful thing, but truly no grown man can attain it without a special gift of grace from God. By Saint Olav—you must remember that the holy king himself did not receive that gift until near the end of his life down here—likely it was God’s will that first he should beget that able boy King Magnus, who threw back the heathens’ storm in the northern lands. King Olav did not have that son by his queen, and yet he sits among the highest saints in the Kingdom of Heaven. Yes, I see from your face that you think this unseemly speech—”
> Sir Baard interrupted:
> “Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, I liked this matter no better than you when Erlend first came to me and said he had set his heart upon a maiden who was promised away. But since then I have understood that there is so strong a love between these two young people that it would be a great sin to sunder their friendship. Erlend was with me at the last Christmas feast King Haakon held for his men; there they met, and as soon as they saw each other, your daughter swooned and lay as dead for a long while—but I saw from my foster son that he would rather lose his life than her.”
> Lavrans sat for a while before replying:
> “Yes, such a thing seems beautiful when one hears it in a chivalric romance from the southern lands. But we are not in Britain, and surely you too would demand more of a man you were to take as son-in-law than that he had caused your daughter to swoon with love before all people’s eyes—”
> The other two were silent; then Lavrans continued:
> “I think this, good sirs: had Erlend Nikulaussøn not greatly diminished both his property and his reputation, you would not be sitting here entreating a man in my circumstances to give his daughter to him. But I do not wish it said of Kristin that she was honored when she married into Husaby with a man of one of the country’s noblest lineages—after the man had disgraced himself so that he could expect no better marriage or uphold the standing of his lineage.”
> He rose violently, went out onto the floor, and paced up and down.
> But Sir Munan sprang up:
> “No, Lavrans, if you speak of disgracing oneself, then by God you must know that you are far too proud—”
> Sir Baard interrupted him; he went over to Lavrans:
> “Proud you are, Lavrans—you are like those farmers we have heard of in former times who would not accept titles from kings because their pride could not endure having people say they owed thanks to anyone but themselves. I tell you, if Erlend possessed all the honor and wealth to which the lad was born, I still would not think I demeaned myself by asking a well-born and prosperous man to give his daughter to my foster son if I understood that the two young people would suffer heartbreak if they were parted. Especially,” he said softly, laying a hand on the other man’s shoulder, “if matters stood such that it was best for the salvation of both their souls that they marry one another.”
> Lavrans drew away from the other man’s hand; his face became closed and cold:
> “I do not believe I understand what you mean, sir.”
> The two men looked at each other for a while; then Sir Baard said:
> “I mean that Erlend has told me the two have sworn themselves to each other with the most solemn oaths. Perhaps you think you have authority to release your child, since she swore without your consent. But you cannot release Erlend.—And I can see nothing other than that what stands most in the way is your pride—and your hatred of sin. But in this matter it seems to me that you wish to be stricter than God Himself, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn!”
> Lavrans replied somewhat uncertainly:
> “Perhaps you have some right in what you say to me here, Sir Baard. But chiefly I have opposed this because it seemed to me that Erlend was an unreliable man in whose hands to place my daughter.”
> “I think I dare stand surety for my foster son now,” said Baard quietly. “He holds Kristin so dear that I know if you give her to him, he will conduct himself so that you shall have no cause to complain of your son-in-law.”
> Lavrans did not answer at once. Then Sir Baard said insistently, holding out his hand:
> “In God’s name, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn, give your word of consent!”
> Lavrans put his hand in Sir Baard’s:
> “In God’s name!”
> Ragnfrid and Kristin were summoned up into the loft, and Lavrans told them his decision. Sir Baard greeted the two women graciously; Sir Munan clasped hands with Ragnfrid and spoke courteously to the mistress, but he greeted Kristin in the foreign manner with a kiss, and he took his time over that greeting. Kristin felt her father watching her during it.
> “How do you like your new kinsman, Sir Munan?” he asked mockingly when he was alone with her for a moment that evening.
> Kristin looked beseechingly at her father. Then he stroked her face several times and said no more.
> When Sir Baard and Sir Munan went to rest, the latter said:
> “I would give not a little to see this Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn’s expression if he learned the truth about this precious daughter. Here you and I have had to beg on our knees that Erlend may have as wife a woman whom he has had with him in Brynhild’s house many times—”
> “Hold your tongue about that,” replied Sir Baard bitterly. “That is the worst thing Erlend has done, that he enticed this child to such places—and never let Lavrans get wind of it; it is best for everyone that those two can now become friends.”
> It was agreed that the betrothal ale should be drunk that same autumn. Lavrans said he could not make the feast very large because the previous year had been one of famine in the valley; in return, he himself would bear the cost of the wedding and hold it at Jørundgaard with seemly splendor. Again he cited the famine year as his reason for requiring the betrothal to last one year.
> VI.
> The betrothal feast was postponed for various reasons; it was not held until after the New Year, but Lavrans agreed that the wedding should not therefore be postponed; it was to take place just after Michaelmas, as the first agreement had stated.
> Thus Kristin sat at Jørundgaard as Erlend’s lawful betrothed. Together with her mother she examined all the goods that had been gathered for her and labored to increase still further the piles of bedding and clothing, for Lavrans wished nothing to be spared once he had given his daughter to the master of Husaby.
> Kristin herself wondered that she was not happier now. But despite all the bustle, there was no true joy at Jørundgaard.
> Her parents sorely missed Ulvhild; she understood that. But she also understood that this was not the only reason they were so quiet and unhappy. They were kind to her, but when they spoke to her of her betrothed, she understood that they forced themselves and did it to please her and show her kindness; they did not do it because they themselves wished to speak of Erlend. They had not become more pleased with the marriage she was making now that they had come to know the man. Erlend himself had also been quiet and reserved during the brief time he had been at Jørundgaard for the betrothal—and it could hardly be otherwise, Kristin thought; he knew that her father had given his consent only reluctantly.
> She and Erlend had scarcely exchanged ten words in private. And it had been strange and unfamiliar to both of them to sit together thus in everyone’s sight; then they had had little to speak of, because they had shared so much in secret. A vague and obscure fear dawned within her, yet was always present—that in some way it might become difficult for them when they were married, because first they had stood far too close to each other and afterward had been wholly separated for far too long.
> But she tried to put it from her. Erlend was meant to visit them at Jørundgaard around Whitsuntide; he had asked Lavrans and Ragnfrid whether they would object to his coming, and Lavrans had laughed a little and replied that Erlend must know he would receive his son-in-law well.
> At Whitsuntide they could walk outside together; then they could talk as in the old days, and then surely this shadow that had come between them through the long separation, while each had gone alone and borne his burden alone, would pass away.
> At Easter Simon Andressøn and his wife were at Formo. Kristin saw them in church. Simon’s wife stood not far from her.
> She must be much older than he, Kristin thought—nearly thirty years old. Lady Halfrid was slight, small, and thin, but she had a wondrously lovely face. Even the pale brown color of her hair, which waved out from beneath the linen cloth, was gentle, as it were, and her eyes were full of gentleness; they were large and gray, sprinkled with tiny flecks of gold. Every feature of her face was fine and pure—but her skin was somewhat grayish pale, and when she opened her mouth one saw that her teeth were not good. She did not look strong and was also said to be sickly—she had apparently already miscarried several times, Kristin heard. She wondered how Simon fared with this wife.
> The people from Jørundgaard and those from Formo had greeted one another across the churchyard slope several times, but had not spoken. On the third day, however, Simon was at church without his wife. Then he went over to Lavrans, and they spoke together for a while. Kristin heard Ulvhild mentioned between them. Afterward he spoke with Ragnfrid. Ramborg, who stood beside her mother, said quite loudly: “I remember you well—I know who you are.” Simon lifted the child slightly and whirled her around. “That was kind of you, Ramborg, not to have forgotten me.” He greeted Kristin only from some distance away. And her parents did not mention the meeting afterward.
> But Kristin thought a great deal about it. It had nevertheless been strange to see Simon Darre again as a married man. So much from the past came alive through it: she remembered her own blind and submissive love for Erlend at that time. Now it was somewhat different. She wondered whether Simon had told his wife how he and she had parted—but she knew he had not done so, “for my father’s sake,” she thought mockingly. It was strangely pitiful that she still went unmarried at home with her parents. But they were betrothed; Simon could see that they had managed to force their will through. Whatever else Erlend had done, he had held faithfully to her, and she had not been loose or wanton.
> One evening early in spring, Ragnfrid needed to send a message south to Old Gunhild, the widow who sewed furs. The evening was so beautiful, and Kristin asked whether she might go; at last she received permission, since all the men were busy.
> It was after sunset, and a fine white frost haze rose toward the gold-green sky. With every hoofbeat Kristin heard the brittle sound of evening ice breaking and splashing out with a rustle. But from the brushwood along the road the birdsong rejoiced softly and springlike, filling the twilight.
> Kristin rode swiftly downward; she was not thinking much about anything, but merely felt that it was good to be outside alone once again. She rode with her eyes fixed on the new moon, which was sinking toward the mountain ridge on the other side of the valley. Thus she nearly fell from the horse when it suddenly sprang aside and reared.
> She saw a dark body lying bundled up at the roadside—at first she was afraid. She was never rid of the ugly fear she had acquired of meeting people alone on the road. But she thought it might be a traveler who had fallen ill, so when she had regained control of the horse, she turned it and rode back, calling out whether anyone was there.
> The bundle moved a little, and a voice said:
> “I believe it is you yourself, Kristin Lavransdatter—”
> “Brother Edvin?” she asked softly. She was almost ready to believe this was an illusion or devilry meant to deceive her. But she went over to him; it truly was the old man, and he could not rise without help.
> “Dear Father—are you out wandering at this time of year?” she said in surprise.
> “Praised be God who sent you this way tonight,” said the monk. Kristin noticed that he was trembling all over. “I intended to go north to you, but now I could walk no farther tonight. I almost believed it was God’s will that I should lie and die on the roads where I have lain and roamed all my life. But I would gladly have had confession and the last rites. And I wished to see you once more, my daughter—”
> Kristin helped the monk onto her horse; she led it by the bridle and supported him. Between lamenting that she was now getting her feet wet in the icy slush, he complained softly of pain.
> He told her that he had been at Eyabu since Christmas; some rich farmers in the district had promised during the famine to improve their church with new ornaments. But the work had proceeded slowly; he had been ill during the winter—there was some ailment in his belly, so that he vomited blood and could not tolerate food. He himself believed he did not have long to live, and he now yearned to return to his monastery; he would prefer to die there among his brothers. But first he had conceived a desire to come north through the valley one last time, and so he had accompanied the priest-monk from Hamar who traveled north to become the new master of the pilgrims’ hospice at Roaldstad. From Fron he had continued alone.
> “I heard that you had become betrothed,” he said, “to this man—. And then I felt such a longing to see you. It grieved me sorely that our last meeting should be the one outside our church. It has lain so heavily upon my heart, Kristin, that you had entered upon the road of discord—”
> Kristin kissed the monk’s hand and said:
> “I do not understand, Father, what I have done or deserved that you show me such great love.”
> The monk replied softly:
> “I have often thought, Kristin, that if matters had so come about that we met more often, you might have become my spiritual daughter.”
> “Do you mean that then you would have led me to turn my mind toward convent life?” asked Kristin. A little later she said: “Sira Eirik laid it upon me that if I could not obtain my father’s consent and marry Erlend, then I must enter a devout sisterhood and make amends for my sins—”
> “I have often prayed that you might feel a longing for convent life,” said Brother Edvin. “But not since you said what you know. I wished you to come to God with your maiden’s wreath, Kristin—”
> When they reached Jørundgaard, Brother Edvin had to be carried inside and put to bed. They placed him in the old winter room, the hearth-room, and tended him as well as they could. He was very ill, and Sira Eirik came over and attended him with remedies for body and soul. But the priest said that the old man suffered from cancer and could not have long to live. Brother Edvin himself thought that once he had recovered a little strength, he would travel south again and try to reach his monastery. Sira Eirik told the others that he did not think this possible.
> Everyone at Jørundgaard felt that a great peace and joy had entered with the monk. People came and went in the hearth-room all day, and there was never a lack of someone willing to keep watch at night beside the sick man. As many as had time crowded together to sit and listen when Sira Eirik came over and read devotional books to the dying man, and when they spoke with Brother Edvin about spiritual matters. And though much of what he said was dark and obscure, as he had always been accustomed to speak, people felt that he strengthened and comforted their souls, because everyone could understand that Brother Edvin was wholly filled with love for God.
> But the monk also wished to hear of all manner of other things, asked for news from the districts, and had Lavrans tell him about the famine year. Some people had resorted to evil counsel in their distress and turned to helpers whom Christian men must shun. A little way up the ridge west of the valley there was a place in the mountain with some large white stones shaped like the private parts of human beings, and some men had fallen to offering rams and kids at this abomination. Sira Eirik had then taken some of the most devout and courageous farmers there one night, and they had broken the stones to pieces. Lavrans had been with them and could testify that they were smeared all over with blood, and that bones and such things lay around them.—Up in Heidal, people were said to have made an old woman sit outside upon an earthfast stone and chant ancient spells on three Thursday nights.
> One night Kristin sat alone with Brother Edvin.
> Around midnight he awoke and seemed to suffer great pain. Then he asked Kristin to take the book about the Virgin Mary’s miracles, which Sira Eirik had lent Brother Edvin, and read to him.
> Kristin was unaccustomed to reading aloud, but she sat on the bed step and placed the candle beside her; she took the book on her lap and read as well as she could.
> After a while she saw the sick man lying with his teeth clenched; he knotted his wasted hands during the fits of pain.
> “You suffer grievously, dear Father,” said Kristin sorrowfully.
> “It seems so to me now. But I know it is God who has made me a child again and tosses me up and down—.
> “—I remember once when I was little—I was four winters old—I had slipped away from home into the forest. I became lost there and was out for many nights and days—. My mother was among the people who found me, and when she lifted me into her arms, she bit me on the back of the neck, I remember. I thought it was because she was angry with me, but later I understood better.
> “—Now I myself long to come home from this forest. It is written: forsake all things and follow Me, but there has been far too much in this world that I had no desire to forsake—”
> “You, Father?” said Kristin. “I have always heard everyone say that you have been an example of chastity and poverty and humility—”
> The monk laughed softly:
> “Yes, you young child surely think there is nothing else in the world that tempts but lust and riches and power. I tell you, these are small things one finds beside the road, and I—I loved the roads—not the world’s small things did I love, but the whole world. God gave me grace to love Lady Poverty and Lady Chastity from my youth onward, and so I thought that with these play-sisters I traveled safely; and then I wandered and walked, wishing I could cross all the roads of the earth. And my heart and thoughts wandered and walked too—I fear I often went astray in my thoughts concerning the darkest matters. But now it is over, little Kristin; now I shall go home to my house and lay aside all my own thoughts and hear the guardian’s clear words about what I am to believe and think concerning my sin and the grace of God—”
> A little later he fell asleep. Kristin sat by the hearth and tended the fire. But toward morning, when she herself was beginning to doze, Brother Edvin suddenly spoke to her from the bed.
> “I am glad, Kristin, that this matter between Erlend Nikulaussøn and you has been brought to a good end.”
> Then Kristin burst into tears:
> “We did so much wrong before we came here. And what gnaws most at my heart is that I caused my father so much sorrow. He is not pleased with this even now. And yet he does not know—if he knew everything, he would surely take his friendship entirely away from me.”
> “Kristin,” said Brother Edvin gently, “do you not understand, child, that this is why you must be silent before him, and why you must cause him no more sorrow—because he will never demand amends from you? Nothing you could do could change your father’s heart toward you.”
> A few days later Brother Edvin was well enough that he wished to travel south. Since this lay so greatly upon his heart, Lavrans had a kind of litter made, suspended between two horses, and thus he conveyed the sick man as far south as Lidstad; there Brother Edvin received fresh horses and a new escort, and in this way he was carried onward as far as Hamar. There he died in the monastery of the preaching friars and was buried in their church. Later the barefoot friars demanded that the corpse be handed over to them, because many people around the districts regarded him as a holy man and spoke of him as Saint Even; farmers prayed to him throughout the Uplands and the Valleys as far north as Trondheim. There was therefore a long dispute between the two monasteries over the corpse.
> Kristin did not hear of this until much later. But she grieved sorely when she parted from the monk. It seemed to her that he alone knew her entire life—he had known the innocent child she had been under her father’s protection. And he had known of her secret life with Erlend, so that he was like a clasp, she thought, binding everything she had held dear to what now filled her mind. Now she was wholly separated from herself as she had been when she was a maiden.
> VII.
> “I think,” said Ragnfrid, feeling the lukewarm brew in the vats, “that now it has cooled enough for us to mix in the yeast.”
> Kristin had been sitting in the brewhouse doorway spinning while she waited for the wort to cool. She laid the spindle on the threshold stone, unwound the pail of dissolved yeast from its covering, and measured it out.
> “Close the door first,” her mother asked, “so no draft comes in.—You go about as though asleep, Kristin,” she said irritably.
> Kristin strained the yeast into the brewing vats while Ragnfrid stirred.
> —Geirhild Drivsdatter called upon Hatt, but it was Odin. Then he came and helped her with the brewing of the ale; as his payment he demanded what lay between the vat and her.—It was a tale Lavrans had once told when she was little.
> —What lay between the vat and her.—Kristin felt sick and dizzy from the heat and the sweet, spiced vapor in the dark, closed brewhouse.
> Outside in the courtyard Ramborg went in a ring with a group of children and sang:
> The eagle sits on the highest hall
> and crooks his gilded claw—
> Kristin followed her mother out through the little anteroom where empty ale barrels and all manner of tools lay. From there a door opened onto a strip of ground between the rear wall of the brewhouse and the split-rail fence around the barley field. A group of pigs shoved one another, bit, and squealed as they fought over the warm spent grain that had been thrown out.
> Kristin shaded her eyes with her hand against the dazzling midday sunlight. Her mother looked at the herd of swine and said:
> “We cannot manage with fewer than eighteen reindeer.”
> “Can we need so many, do you think?” said her daughter absently.
> “Yes, we must serve game with the pork every day,” her mother replied. “And of birds and hares we shall scarcely have more than what will be consumed in the upper loft-room alone. You must remember that nearly two hundred people will come here—with servants and children—and the poor who are to be fed. And even if you and Erlend leave on the fifth day, some of the guests will surely stay out the week—at least.”
> “You stay here and watch the ale, Kristin,” said Ragnfrid. “I must go and see to the food for your father and the haymakers.”
> Kristin fetched her spinning and sat down there in the back doorway. She tucked the distaff with its head of wool into her armpit, but her hands sank into her lap with the spindle.
> Beyond the split-rail fence the ears of barley gleamed like silver and silk in the sun. Above the rushing of the river she now and then heard the sound of scythes in the meadows out on the island—sometimes the iron struck a stone. Her father and the household were struggling to finish the haymaking. There was so much to labor over for her wedding.
> The smell of the warm spent grain and the sharp stench of the swine—she grew nauseated again. And the midday heat made her so dizzy and faint. White and rigid in the back, she sat waiting for it to pass—she did not want to be sick again.
> —She had never felt like this before. It was no use trying to comfort herself; it was not uncertain any longer, she could not be mistaken. What lay between the vat and her—.
> Eighteen reindeer. Nearly two hundred wedding guests.—People would have something to laugh at when it became known that all this commotion had merely been about a woman with child whom they must see married beforehand—.
> Oh no. She threw aside her spinning and sprang up.
> With her forehead against the brewhouse wall, she retched into the nettle thicket growing thickly beside it. Brown caterpillars swarmed over the nettles—the sight made her still sicker.
> Kristin passed her hands over her sweat-wet temples.
> Oh no, it was certain enough—.
> They were to be married on the second Sunday after Michaelmas, and then their wedding was to be celebrated for five days. There were more than two months remaining. By then they would surely be able to see it upon her—her mother and the other matrons in the district. They were always so wise about such things, always knew when a woman went with child months before Kristin could understand what they saw it by. Poor thing, how she is fading—. Impatiently Kristin rubbed her hands against her cheeks, for she felt they were bloodlessly white.
> Before, she had so often thought that this must surely happen at some time. And she had not been so terribly afraid of it. But it would not have been the same then, when they could not and were not permitted to have each other lawfully. It was reckoned—yes, as a shame in a way, and as sin too—but when two young people would not allow themselves to be forced apart, people did not forget that either and spoke of them with kindness. She would not have been ashamed. But when such a thing happened between betrothed people—then it was merely laughed at and coarsely jested about. She herself understood—it was ridiculous; here they brewed and mixed wine, animals were to be slaughtered, bread baked, and preparations made for a wedding people would speak of—and she, the bride, grew nauseated merely from smelling food, crept behind the outbuildings in a cold sweat, and was sick—.
> Erlend. She clenched her teeth in anger. He should have spared her this. For she had not wanted it. He ought to have remembered that before, when everything had been so uncertain for her, when she had had nothing to rely upon but his love, then she had always, always gladly yielded to his will. He should have left her be now, when she tried to refuse because she thought it was not beautiful of them to steal something for themselves after her father had joined their hands in the sight of both their kindreds. But he had taken her, half by force, with laughter and caresses; so that she had not been able to show him that she had meant her resistance in earnest.
> —She went inside and looked to the ale, came back, and stood leaning over the split-rail fence. The grain swayed faintly in a little breath of air and gleamed. She could not remember ever seeing the fields so thick and luxuriant as this year.—The river flashed far out, and she heard her father’s voice calling—she could not distinguish the words, but the people out on the island laughed.
> —What if she went to her father and told him.—It was better to abandon all this labor, join her and Erlend quietly, without church consecration and a great feast—now that it was merely a matter of her receiving the name of wife before it became visible to every person that she already carried Erlend’s child beneath her breast.
> Erlend would be mocked too, as fully as she—or more; he was no young boy. But it was he who had wanted this wedding; he had wished to see her stand as bride in silk and velvet and a tall golden crown—he had wished for that, and he had wished to possess her too in all the sweet, secret hours. She had yielded to him in every single thing. She would continue to do his will in the other matter too.
> Then in the end he would see—no one could have both things. He who had spoken of the great Christmas feast he would hold at Husaby the first year she sat as mistress of his estate—then he would display to all his kinsmen and friends and people from the districts far and wide the beautiful wife he had acquired. Kristin smiled mockingly. That would hardly be fitting around Christmas this year.
> Around Saint Gregory’s Mass it would be. It was as though her thoughts began to swarm when she told herself that around Saint Gregory’s Mass she would give birth. She was somewhat afraid of that too—she remembered her mother’s screams ringing over the farm for two days when Ulvhild came into the world. At Ulvsvolden two young women, one after the other, had died in childbirth—and both the first wives of Sigurd of Loptsgaard. And her own grandmother, after whom she had been named—.
> But she was not chiefly afraid. During these years she had often thought, whenever she noticed anew that she was still not with child—perhaps this would be the punishment upon her and Erlend. That she would remain barren. They would come to wait and wait in vain for what they had once feared, would come to hope as futilely as before they had feared needlessly. Until at last they knew that one day they would be carried out of his ancestral estate and be gone—his brother was a priest, and the children Erlend had could never inherit him. Munan Stump and his sons would enter and take their seats in their place, and Erlend would be erased from the lineage.
> She pressed one hand hard against her lap. It was there—between the split-rail fence and her, between the vat and her. Between her and the whole world it was—Erlend’s lawful son. She had already tried the method she had once heard Lady Aashild speak of, with blood from the right and left arms. It was a son she carried. Whatever he might bring her.—She remembered her dead little brothers, her parents’ sorrowful faces whenever they mentioned them; she remembered all the times she had seen them despair because of Ulvhild—the night Ulvhild died. And she thought of all the sorrow she herself had caused them, of her father’s grief-worn face—and still it was not the end of the sorrows she would bring upon Father and Mother—.
> Nevertheless, nevertheless. Kristin laid her head upon her arm over the split-rail fence; her other hand she still held over her lap. If it brought her new sorrows, if it should force her to death—she would rather die to bear Erlend a son than that they should both die one day and the houses stand empty after them and grain sway for strangers across their fields—.
> Someone came into the anteroom. The ale! thought Kristin—I should have looked to it long ago. She straightened—then Erlend ducked beneath the lintel and came out into the sunlight, radiant with joy:
> “Is this where you stand?” he said. “And you do not take even one step to meet me?” he asked her; he came over and put his arms around her.
> “Dear heart, have you come here?” she said in surprise.
> He must just have dismounted—he still had his cloak over his shoulder and his sword at his side—he was disheveled, dirty, and very dusty. He was dressed in a red tunic that fell in folds from the neckline and was slit at the sides nearly to beneath his arms. As they walked through the brewhouse and up the courtyard, the garment flapped around him so that his thighs were visible all the way to his waist. It was strange—she had never before noticed that his feet turned slightly outward when he walked—before she had only seen that he had long, slender legs, fine ankles, and small, well-shaped feet.
> Erlend had come with a strong company—with five retainers and four spare horses. He told Ragnfrid that he had come to fetch Kristin’s property—would it not be pleasanter for her to find her things at Husaby when she arrived? And since the wedding was to be so late in autumn, it might be harder to transport them then—they could easily be spoiled by seawater aboard the ship too. Now the abbot of Nidarholm had offered to let him send them on the Laurentius ship—they expected it to sail from Veøy around the Feast of the Assumption. Therefore he had come to have the goods driven through Raumsdal to the headland.
> He sat in the cookhouse doorway drinking ale and talking while Ragnfrid and Kristin plucked the wild ducks Lavrans had brought home the previous day. Mother and daughter were alone by the houses; the women were in the meadow raking. He looked so happy—well pleased with himself for having come on so sensible an errand.
> Her mother went out, and Kristin tended the spit with the birds. Through the open door she could glimpse the band of retainers lying in the shade on the other side of the courtyard and passing the ale bowl around. He himself sat on the threshold, chatting and laughing—the sun shone directly on his bare, soot-black hair; she discovered that there were some gray strands in it. Yes, he was nearly thirty-two, surely—but he behaved like a high-spirited boy. She knew she would not tell him of her difficulty—soon enough, when he saw it himself. Laughing tenderness flowed in her mind over the little hard anger at the bottom, like a glittering river over stones.
> She loved him beyond everything—it filled her mind, even though all the while she saw and remembered everything else. How little this courtly man in his fine red tunic, with silver spurs on his feet and a gold-adorned belt, suited the harvest bustle here at Jørundgaard.—She also noticed that her father did not come up to the farm, although her mother had sent Ramborg down to the river with word of what guest had arrived.
> Erlend stood beside her and put his arms around her shoulders:
> “Can you understand it?” he said radiantly. “Does it not seem strange to you—that it is our wedding all this labor is for?”
> Kristin gave him a kiss and pushed him away—ladled fat over the birds and told him not to stand in the way. No, she would not tell him—.
> Lavrans did not come up to the farm until supper time—together with the haymakers. He was dressed little differently from the laborers, in an undyed, knee-length homespun tunic and ankle-length breeches of the same cloth; he went barefoot and carried his scythe over his shoulder. The only way in which his clothing differed from the servants’ was that he wore a leather shoulder guard for the falcon sitting on his left shoulder. He led Ramborg by the hand.
> Lavrans greeted his son-in-law cordially enough and asked him to excuse his not having come sooner—they had to work as hard as they could at the farm labor, because he himself was obliged to make a journey to town between haymaking and harvest. But when Erlend stated his errand over the meal table, Lavrans became rather surly.
> He could not possibly spare wagons and horses now. Erlend replied that he had brought four spare horses himself. Lavrans thought there would be at least three loads. Besides, the maiden had to keep her ordinary clothing here. And the bedclothes Kristin was to take with her were needed on the farm during the wedding, with so many guests to lodge.
> No, no, Erlend thought. Surely there would be a way to transport it in autumn too. But he had been pleased and thought it sounded sensible when the abbot suggested it could go on the monastery ship. The abbot had recalled the kinship: “They all remember it now,” said Erlend with a smile. His father-in-law’s displeasure did not seem to penetrate him in the least.
> Nevertheless, it was settled that Erlend should borrow one wagon and carry away one load of the things Kristin would need most as soon as she came to her new home.
> The next day, then, they were busy packing. Her mother thought the large and small looms could go now—Kristin would scarcely have time to weave more before the wedding. Ragnfrid and her daughter cut down the cloth that was on the loom. It was undyed homespun, but of the finest, softest wool, with tufts spun in from black sheep that formed regular spots. Kristin and her mother rolled up the cloth and laid it in a leather sack. Kristin thought it would be good for swaddling—beautiful too when red or blue bands were wrapped outside it.
> The sewing stand, which Arne had once forged, could also be sent. Kristin took from the chest all the things Erlend had given her from time to time. She showed her mother the blue velvet cloak with a red pattern that she was to wear on the bridal journey. Her mother turned it this way and that and felt the cloth and fur lining.
> “It has been a very costly cloak,” said Ragnfrid. “When did Erlend give you this?”
> “He gave it to me while I was at Nonneseter,” said her daughter.
> Kristin’s bridal chest, the one her mother had filled since she was little, was repacked. It was carved into squares, with a leaping animal or bird amid foliage in each panel. Ragnfrid placed her bridal gown in one of her own chests. It was not quite finished; they had sewn on it during the winter. It was of scarlet silk and cut to fit very closely around the body. Kristin thought it would now be far too tight across her breasts.
> Toward evening the load stood ready and strapped beneath the wagon cover. Erlend was to leave early the next morning.
> He stood with Kristin leaning over the farm gate, looking northward, where blue-black storm-darkness filled the valley. Thunder rolled within the mountain—but southward the fields and river lay in yellow, piercing sunlight.
> “Can you remember the storm that day in the forest by Gerdarud?” he asked softly, playing with her fingers.
> Kristin nodded and tried to smile. The air was so heavy and close—her head ached, and she sweated with every breath she took.
> Lavrans came over to the two at the gate and spoke about the weather. It seldom did damage down here in the district—but God must know whether one might hear of disasters to the livestock and horses up in the mountains.
> It was black as night above the church on the hill. A flash of lightning revealed a herd of horses standing crowded together and restless on the field outside the churchyard gate. Lavrans did not think they belonged here in the district—they were more likely horses from Dovre that had grazed in the mountains beneath Jetta; nevertheless he felt inclined to go up and look at them, he shouted through the thunder—lest there be some of his among them—.
> A terrible flash of lightning ripped apart the darkness above—the thunder cracked and crashed so that they could not hear the sound of their own voices. The herd of horses burst across the fields beneath the ridge. All three crossed themselves—
> —then another flash came; it was as though the sky split open directly overhead, a vast snow-white flame swept down upon them—the three were flung against one another; they stood with closed, blinded eyes and smelled something like scorched stone—and the thunderclap deafened their ears.
> “Saint Olav, help us,” said Lavrans softly. “Look at the birch, look at the birch,” screamed Erlend: the great birch in the field seemed to sway—then a huge branch tore free and sank toward the ground, breaking a gash from the trunk.
> “Do you think it will catch fire—Jesus Christ! The church roof is burning,” cried Lavrans.
> They stood staring—no—yes! Red flames seeped out of the shingle covering beneath the roof turret.
> Both men began to run back across the courtyard.
> Lavrans tore open every house door and shouted inside; people poured out.
> “Take axes, take axes—the timber axes,” he cried, “and hooks—” He rushed past to the stable. A moment later he came out leading Guldsveinen by the mane; he sprang onto the unsaddled horse and galloped northward, holding the great broad axe in his hand. Erlend rode directly after him—all the men followed; some were mounted, but some could not control the frightened animals, gave up, and ran after them. Last came Ragnfrid and the farm’s women with buckets and pails.
> No one seemed aware of the storm any longer. In the flashes of lightning they saw people streaming out of the houses farther down in the district. Sira Eirik was already running up the slope, followed by his household. Horse hooves thundered on the bridge below—some fellows galloped past; they turned white, terrified faces toward their burning church.
> A little wind blew from the southeast. The fire had a strong hold on the north wall; on the west, the entrance door was already blocked. But it had not yet caught the south side or the rounded chancel.
> Kristin and the women from Jørundgaard entered the graveyard south of the church at a place where the fence had been broken down.
> The vast red glare lit the grove north of the church and the place with the rails for tethering horses. No one could approach there because of the heat—the cross stood alone in front, bathed in the light of the flames. It looked as though it lived and moved.
> Through the roaring and whining of the fire came the blows of axes against the timbers of the south wall. Men were in the gallery chopping and hammering, while others tried to tear down the covered passage itself. Someone shouted to the women from Jørundgaard that Lavrans and a few other men had followed Sira Eirik into the church. They must see about making an opening in the wall—little tongues of flame played between the shingles of the roof on this side too. If the wind shifted or died down, the fire would close around the entire church.
> It was useless to think of extinguishing it; there was no time to form a chain down to the river, but at Ragnfrid’s command the women formed a line and passed water from the little stream running west along the roadside—there was enough to throw a little on the south wall and on the men working there. Many of them sobbed as they did so, from strain and fear for the people trapped inside the burning building, and from grief for their church.
> Kristin stood at the very front of the women’s line passing buckets—she stared breathlessly at the church where both her father and Erlend were surely inside.
> The pillars of the covered passage lay torn down in a tangle of wood and sheets of shingles from the roof of the ambulatory. The men attacked the timber wall with all their strength—a whole group had lifted a log and were charging against it.
> Erlend and one of his retainers came out through the little door on the south side of the chancel; between them they carried the large chest from the sacristy—the one Eirik used to sit upon when he heard confessions. Erlend and the man overturned the chest in the churchyard.
> Kristin could not hear what he shouted; he ran back into the gallery. He was supple as a cat as he ran there—he had thrown off his outer garments and ran in only shirt, breeches, and hose.
> The others repeated his cry—the sacristy and chancel were burning; no one could pass from the nave to the south door any longer—the fire now blocked both exits. Several timbers in the wall had been splintered; Erlend had taken a fire hook and tore and pulled at the wreckage of the timbers—they stripped open a hole in the side of the church, while other people cried that they must beware, for the roof might slide down and trap those inside the church; the shingled roof was burning briskly on this side now too, and the heat was becoming unbearable.
> Erlend sprang into the hole and helped Sira Eirik out. The priest came with the skirt of his robe full of the altars’ holy vessels. A young boy followed with his hand over his face and the tall processional cross projecting before him. Lavrans came after them. He kept his eyes closed against the smoke—he staggered beneath the great crucifix he carried in his arms; it was much taller than the man.
> People ran forward and helped them down into the churchyard. Sira Eirik stumbled, fell to his knees, and the altar vessels rolled down the slope. The silver dove sprang open, and the Host fell out—the priest picked it up, brushed it off, and kissed it while sobbing aloud; he kissed the gilded man’s head that had stood above the altar with a little of Saint Olav’s hair and fingernails inside it.
> Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn still stood holding the rood cross. His arm lay across the arms of the cross; he leaned his head against the shoulder of the image of Christ; it looked as though the Savior bent His fair, sorrowful face comfortingly toward the man.
> The roof had begun collapsing in sections on the north side of the church—a burning piece of beam was flung out and struck the great bell in the belfry by the churchyard gate. The bell sounded with a deep sobbing note that died in a long groan drowned by the roar of the fire.
> No one had heeded the weather during this—the whole event had not lasted long, but people had probably not perceived that either. Now it thundered and flashed far south in the valley; the rain that had been falling for some time increased, and the wind died.
> But suddenly it was as though a sail of flame were hoisted from the sill—one instant, and with a shriek the fire closed around the church from end to end.
> People fled from the devouring heat. Erlend was suddenly at Kristin’s side and drew her downward. All of him smelled scorched—her hand filled with burned hair when she stroked his head and face. They could not hear each other’s voices over the roar of the fire. But she saw that his eyebrows were singed away to the roots, that he had burns on his face, and that there were burned places in his shirt. He laughed as he drew her with him after the others.
> The people followed the weeping old priest and Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn with the crucifix.
> At the edge of the churchyard Lavrans set down the rood and leaned it against a tree; he sank onto the wreckage of the fence. Sira Eirik was already sitting there—he stretched out his arms toward the burning church:
