> “Farewell, farewell, you Church of Saint Olav. God bless you, my Church of Saint Olav, God bless you for every hour I have sung in you and said Mass—you Church of Saint Olav, good night, good night—”
> The parishioners wept aloud with him. Rain streamed down over the groups of people, but no one thought of leaving. It did not seem to dampen the heat in the tarred woodwork—brands and glowing shingles were flung around. A moment later the roof turret plunged into the fire with a cloud of sparks rising after it.
> Lavrans sat with one hand over his face; his other arm lay across his lap, and Kristin saw that the sleeve was bloody from the shoulder all the way down; blood ran over his fingers. She went over and touched him.
> “It is surely not much—something fell upon my shoulder,” he said and looked up. He was white to the lips. “Ulvhild,” he whispered in anguish, looking into the blaze.
> Sira Eirik heard it and placed a hand on his shoulder.
> “It will not awaken your child, Lavrans—she sleeps just as well though it burns above her resting place,” he said. “She has not lost the home of her soul, as the rest of us have tonight.”
> Kristin hid her face against Erlend’s breast—stood thus and felt his arms around her shoulders. Then she heard her father ask for his wife.
> Someone replied that a woman had been seized by labor pains from fright; they had carried her down to the rectory. And Ragnfrid had gone with her there.
> Kristin remembered again what she had wholly forgotten from the moment they learned that the church was burning. She surely should not have looked upon this. There was a man south in the district who had a red mark over half his face; they said he had become like that because his mother had looked upon a wildfire while she carried him.
> Dear, holy Virgin Mary, she prayed inwardly, let my unborn child not have taken harm from this—.
> The next day a district assembly was summoned on the church slope—the people were to take counsel about rebuilding the church.
> Kristin sought out Sira Eirik at Romundgaard before he was to go to the assembly. She asked the priest whether he thought she should take this as a sign. Perhaps it was God’s will that she tell her father she was unworthy to stand beneath the bridal crown; it was more seemly that she be given to Erlend Nikulaussøn in marriage without an honorable feast.
> But Sira Eirik sprang at her, his eyes glittering with anger:
> “Do you think God cares so greatly how you bitches run about and waste yourselves that He would burn down a fair and honored church for your sake! Put aside your pride and do not cause your mother and Lavrans a sorrow from which they will recover only late. If you do not bear the crown with honor on your day of honor—that is ill for you, but all the more do you and Erlend need consecration when you are joined. Each person has his own sins to answer for; that is surely why this misfortune has come upon us all. See to amending your life, and help rebuild the church here, both you and Erlend.”
> Kristin thought she had not yet told him this latest thing that had happened—but she set her mind at rest with this.
> She went with the men to the assembly. Lavrans came with his arm in a sling, and Erlend had many burns on his face; he looked dreadful, but laughed about it. None of the wounds was large, and he said he hoped they would not disfigure him until he was to be a bridegroom. He rose after Lavrans and promised to offer four marks of silver to the church and, on behalf of his betrothed and with Lavrans’s consent, one mark’s worth of Kristin’s land in the parish.
> Erlend had to remain at Jørundgaard for a week because of his wounds. Kristin saw that Lavrans seemed to have come to like his son-in-law better after the night of the fire; the men now appeared to be firm friends. She thought then that perhaps her father might become so fond of Erlend Nikulaussøn that he would judge leniently and not take it as heavily as she had feared when the time came for him to understand that they had sinned against him.
> VIII.
> That year proved unusually good throughout the northern valley. There was much hay, and it was brought dry into the barns; people returned from the shielings with a great yield of dairy produce and fat herds—and that year they had been mercifully spared from beasts of prey. The grain stood so fair that few people could remember ever seeing it stand better—it ripened well and was full of nourishment, and the weather was the very best. Between Saint Bartholomew’s Mass and the Nativity of Mary, the time when night frosts were most to be feared, it rained a little and was mild and cloudy, but afterward the harvest month passed with sun and wind and mild, hazy nights. In the week after Michaelmas, most of the grain had been gathered throughout the district.
> At Jørundgaard they labored and prepared for the great wedding. During the last two months Kristin had been so busy from morning until evening every single day that she had little time to worry about anything but the work. She saw that she had grown large in the breast; the little pale-pink nipples had turned brown, and they were tender as sores every morning when she had to rise into the cold—but it passed when she had worked herself warm, and then she thought only of everything she had to accomplish before evening. When she had to straighten her back now and then and stand resting for a while, she felt that what she carried in her lap was beginning to grow heavy—but she was still just as narrow and slender to look upon. She passed her hands down over her long, fine hips. No, she would not grieve over it now. At times she thought with a little tingling longing—in a month or so she would surely feel life—. By then she would be at Husaby. Perhaps Erlend would be glad—.
> She closed her eyes and bit her betrothal ring—saw before her Erlend’s face, pale with emotion, when he stood up in the upper loft-room and spoke the betrothal words in a loud, clear voice:
> “So may God be my witness, and these men who stand here, that I, Erlend Nikulaussøn, lawfully betroth myself to Kristin Lavransdatter according to the law of God and men, upon such terms as have here been spoken before these witnesses who stand by. That I shall have you as my wife and you shall have me as your husband, as long as we both live, to dwell together in matrimony, with all such fellowship as the law of God and the law of the land recognize.”
> She ran on errands across the courtyard from house to house and stopped for a moment—the rowan was so full of berries that year; it would be a snowy winter. And the sun shone over the pale fields where the grain stood on drying poles. If only this weather would hold through the wedding.
> Lavrans held firmly to his daughter’s being married in church. It had therefore been decided that this should take place in the chapel at Sundbu. On Saturday the bridal procession was to ride over the mountain to Vaage; they would spend the night at Sundbu and the neighboring farms and ride back on Sunday after the bridal Mass. That same evening after vespers, when the holy day was over, the wedding was to be celebrated and Lavrans would give his daughter to Erlend. After midnight bride and bridegroom were to be escorted to bed together.
> On Friday afternoon Kristin stood in the gallery of the upper loft and watched the procession riding from the north past the burned church on the hill. It was Erlend and all his bridal attendants. She strained her eyes to distinguish him from the others. They must not see one another; no man was now permitted to see her before she was brought forth dressed as a bride the next day.
> Where the road turned off toward Jørundgaard, a few women separated from the company. The men rode onward to Laugar Bridge; they were to sleep there that night.
> Kristin went down to meet the arrivals. She felt so tired after the bath, and her scalp was sore—her mother had used such strong lye water on her hair to make it properly fair for the next day.
> Lady Aashild Gautesdatter slid from the saddle into Lavrans’s arms. How lightly and youthfully she carries herself, thought Kristin. The daughter-in-law, Sir Munan’s wife Katrin, looked almost older; she was large and stout, colorless in skin and eyes. It is strange, Kristin thought, she is ugly and he is unfaithful, and yet people say they live well together.
> Then there were two daughters of Sir Baard Petersøn, one married and one unmarried. They were neither ugly nor beautiful, looked trustworthy and good, but bore themselves rather stiffly toward strangers. Lavrans thanked them courteously because they had wished to do such honor to this wedding and travel so long a road so late in autumn.
> “Erlend was fostered by our father when he was a boy,” said the elder, coming forward and greeting Kristin.
> Now two young men came riding into the courtyard at a sharp trot—they jumped from their horses and ran laughing after Kristin, who fled into the house and hid. They were the young sons of Trond Gjesling, handsome and promising boys. They brought the bridal crown from Sundbu in a casket. Trond and his wife were first to accompany them to Jørundgaard on Sunday after Mass.
> Kristin had fled into the hearth-room; Lady Aashild came after her, put her hands on Kristin’s shoulders, and drew her face down to her own for a kiss.
> “I am glad I shall live to see this day,” said Lady Aashild.
> She saw how thin Kristin’s hands had become, the hands she held. She saw that the bride had grown thin otherwise, but high in the breast. All the features of her face had grown narrower and finer than before; her temples seemed slightly hollowed in the shadow of her heavy, damp hair. Her cheeks were no longer rounded, and the fresh color had faded. But Kristin’s eyes had grown much larger and darker.
> Lady Aashild kissed her again.
> “I see you have had much to labor over, Kristin,” she said. “I shall give you a drink tonight so that you may be rested and fresh tomorrow.”
> Kristin’s lips began to tremble.
> “Hush,” said Lady Aashild, patting her hand. “I look forward to adorning you tomorrow—no one shall ever have seen a more beautiful bride than you will be tomorrow.”
> Lavrans rode over to Laugar Bridge to sit at table with his guests there.
> The men could not praise the food highly enough—one could not obtain better Friday fare in the richest monastery. There was rye-flour porridge, boiled beans, white bread, and for fish they had nothing but trout, salted and fresh, and rich dried halibut.
> As the men took more of the ale, they grew more and more merry and jested more and more coarsely with the bridegroom. All Erlend’s bridal attendants were much younger than he—his own contemporaries and friends had all been married men for many years. Now the men joked that he was so old and was to enter the bridal bed for the first time. Some of Erlend’s older kinsmen, who were still fairly sober, sat fearing at every word that fell that the talk would now drift toward subjects better left untouched. Sir Baard of Hestnæs watched Lavrans.
> Lavrans drank steadily, but the ale did not appear to make him happier as he sat in the high seat—his face grew more and more tense as his eyes grew more fixed. But Erlend, who sat at his father-in-law’s right hand, answered the jests merrily and laughed a great deal; his face was red and his eyes sparkled.
> Suddenly Lavrans flared up:
> “That wagon, son-in-law—while I remember it, what have you done with the wagon you borrowed from me this summer—”
> “Wagon—” said Erlend.
> “Do you no longer remember that you borrowed a wagon from me this summer—? God knows it was so good a wagon that I shall never see a better, for I oversaw it myself when it was forged here on the farm. You promised and swore, I testify before God and my household knows, that you promised to return it to me, but you have not kept that word—”
> Some of the guests called out that this was nothing to speak of now, but Lavrans struck the table and swore that he would know what Erlend had done with his wagon.
> “Oh, it is probably standing at the farm on the headland where we obtained a boat to Veøy,” said Erlend indifferently. “I did not think it mattered so greatly. It is like this, Father-in-law—the journey with the load down through the valleys was long and hard, so when we reached the fjord, none of my men wished to travel the whole road back here with the wagon and then afterward over the mountain north to Trondheim. Then I thought it could surely remain there for the time being—”
> “No, may the devil move me from where I sit if I have ever heard your equal,” Lavrans began. “What sort of custom do you have in your house—is it you or your retainers who decide where they will or will not travel—”
> Erlend shrugged:
> “It is true, much has been otherwise than it should be at my home.—I shall have the wagon sent south to you again when Kristin and I travel that way.—Dear Father-in-law,” he said, smiling and holding out his hand, “you may know that everything and I myself will now be different when I bring Kristin home as mistress. It was ill done with this wagon. But I promise you this shall be the last time you have cause to complain of me.”
> “Dear Lavrans,” begged Baard Petersøn, “be reconciled with him over this small matter—”
> “Small matter or greater matter,” Lavrans began. But then he mastered himself and clasped hands with Erlend.
> Soon afterward he departed, and the guests at Laugar Bridge sought their sleeping places.
> On Saturday before High Mass, wives and maidens were busy in the old loft. Some prepared the bridal bed, and some finished adorning the bride.
> Ragnfrid had chosen this house as the bridal house partly because it was the smallest loft—they could lodge many more guests in the new storehouse loft; they themselves had used this one as a sleeping room in summer when Kristin was little, before Lavrans had built the great dwelling house where they now lived both summer and winter. But the old storehouse was also nearly the most beautiful building on the farm since Lavrans had restored it—it had been close to collapse when they moved into Jørundgaard. It was adorned with the finest woodcarving both outside and within, and though the loft-room was not large, it was all the easier to drape beautifully with tapestries, coverlets, and hides.
> The bridal bed stood ready, made with silk-covered cushions; beautiful tapestries had been hung around it like a tent; over the hides and quilts was spread an embroidered silk cloth. Ragnfrid and several other women were hanging coverlets over the wall logs and laying cushions on the benches.
> Kristin sat in an armchair that had been carried up. She was dressed in her scarlet bridal gown. Large brooches held it together over her breast and closed the yellow silk shift at the neck opening; golden arm rings gleamed upon the yellow silk sleeves. A gilded silver belt was wound three times around her waist, and chain upon chain lay around her neck and breast, outermost her father’s old gold chain with the great reliquary cross. Her hands, lying in her lap, were heavy with rings.
> Lady Aashild stood behind her chair and brushed out the abundant golden-brown hair.
> “Tomorrow you shall wear it loose for the last time,” she said with a smile, winding the red and green silk ribbons meant to support the crown around Kristin’s head. Then the women crowded around the bride.
> Ragnfrid and Gyrid of Skog took the Gjesling lineage’s great bridal crown from the table. It was entirely gilded; the points ended alternately in crosses and clover leaves, and the circlet was set with rock crystals.
> They pressed it down around the bride’s head. Ragnfrid was pale, and her hands trembled as she did so.
> Kristin rose slowly. Jesus, how heavy it was to bear all this gold and silver—. Then Lady Aashild took her by the hand and led her forward to a large vessel of water—while the bridal maidens opened the door onto the sunlight so that the loft would be bright.
> “Look at yourself now, Kristin,” said Lady Aashild, and Kristin bent over the vessel. She dimly saw her own face rise white from the water; it came so near that she saw the golden crown above it. Around it moved so many light and dark shadows in the reflection—there was something she was on the point of remembering—then it was as though she would faint—she seized the rim of the vessel. Lady Aashild laid her hand upon Kristin’s and dug her nails so painfully into the flesh that Kristin came to herself.
> Horn calls sounded down by the bridge. From the farm they called up that the bridegroom was now arriving with his company.
> The matrons led Kristin out onto the storehouse gallery.
> In the courtyard there was a surging of horses in festive harness and people in holy-day clothes; everything glittered and gleamed in the sun. Kristin looked out past it all toward the valley. Her home district lay bright and still beneath a thin, mist-blue haze; from the haze rose the mountains, gray with scree and black with forest, and from a cloudless sky the sun poured its light into the bowl of the valley.
> She had not noticed it before, but all the leaves had fallen from the trees; the groves shone silver-gray and bare. Only the alder brush along the river had a little faded green at the tops, and one birch or another still held some white-yellow leaves on the ends of its twigs. But the trees were nearly bare—except the rowan, which still shone with brown-red leaves around the bloodred berries. In the still, warm day, the ash-colored carpet spread everywhere by fallen leaves gave off the sour scent of autumn.
> Had it not been for the rowan trees, it might have been early spring. And for the stillness—but it was autumn-still, so still. Whenever the horn calls ceased, no other sound could be heard from the district but the clanking of bells in all the stubble fields and fallows where the livestock grazed.
> The river was shrunken and small; it rushed so quietly; there were only a few little threads of water running between sandbanks and broad expanses of white, smoothly rounded stones. No roar of streams came from the slopes—it had been such a dry autumn. Yet wetness gleamed everywhere upon the fields—but it was only the moisture that seeps up from the earth in autumn, however warm the days and clear the air may be.
> The crowd below in the courtyard parted and made way for the bridegroom’s company. The young bridal attendants were just riding forward; there was commotion among the women in the gallery.
> Lady Aashild stood beside the bride:
> “Bear yourself well now, Kristin,” she said. “It will not be long before you are safely beneath the wife’s linen.”
> Kristin nodded helplessly. She felt how terribly white her face must be.
> “I am surely far too pale a bride,” she said softly.
> “You are the most beautiful bride,” replied Aashild, “and there rides Erlend—one would have to search for two more beautiful than you.”
> Erlend himself rode forward beneath the gallery. He sprang from the horse, light and unhindered by the heavy, many-folded clothes. Kristin thought him so beautiful that it hurt throughout her body.
> He was dressed darkly, in an ankle-length, slit silk tunic, withered-brown in color and woven with black and white. Around his waist he wore a gold-adorned belt, and at his left hip a sword with gold on hilt and scabbard. Over his shoulders fell a heavy dark-blue velvet cloak, and upon his black hair he wore a black French silk cap gathered like wings at the sides and ending in two long tails; one had been cast across his breast from the left shoulder and backward.
> Erlend greeted his bride above, went over to her horse, and stood beside it with his hand on the saddle bow while Lavrans climbed the stairs. Kristin felt so strange and dizzy amid all this splendor—her father looked unfamiliar in his solemn, ankle-length green velvet garment. But her mother was gray-pale beneath her linen cloth and wore her red silk gown.
> Ragnfrid came and placed the cloak around her daughter.
> Then Lavrans took the bride’s hand and led her down to Erlend. He lifted her onto the horse and himself mounted. The two sat side by side before the bridal loft while the procession began to ride out through the farm gate. First came the priests: Sira Eirik, Sira Tormod from Ulvsvolden, and a canon from Hamar who was her father’s friend. Then came the bridal attendants and maidens in pairs. And now Erlend and she were to ride forward. After them came the bride’s parents, kinsmen, friends, and guests in a long line down between the split-rail fences to the district road. For a long stretch ahead, clusters of rowan berries, spruce branches, and the last white mayweed of autumn had been strewn, and people stood along the road where the procession was to ride, greeting them with shouts.
> On Sunday just after sunset the mounted procession returned to Jørundgaard. Through the first shreds of twilight the bonfires glowed red in the courtyard of the wedding farm. Entertainers and musicians sang and played fiddles and drums while the company rode toward the warm red glow.
> Kristin nearly collapsed when Erlend lifted her from the horse before the gallery of the upper loft.
> “I was so cold over the mountain,” she whispered. “I am so tired—.” She stood for a while—when she climbed the stairs to the loft, she swayed at every step.
> Up in the upper loft-room, the chilled wedding guests soon warmed their bodies—the many candles burning in the room gave off heat, steaming hot food was carried around, and wine, mead, and strong ale passed from hand to hand. The murmur of voices and the sound of people eating droned far away in Kristin’s ears.
> She sat unable to become properly warm. After a while her cheeks began to burn, but her feet would not thaw, and shivers ran down her back. All the heavy gold forced her forward as she sat in the high seat beside Erlend.
> Whenever the bridegroom drank to her, she had to look at the red patches and spots showing so sharply on his face now that he was growing hot after the ride in the cold. They were marks from the burns of that summer.
> The terrible fear had come upon her the previous evening as they sat at table at Sundbu. When she met Bjørn Gunnarsøn’s extinguished gaze resting upon her and Erlend—the eyes neither blinked nor wavered. They had dressed Sir Bjørn in knightly clothes—he looked like a dead man conjured back to life.
> At night she lay with Lady Aashild—she was the bridegroom’s nearest female kinswoman at the feast.
> “What is the matter with you, Kristin?” said Lady Aashild somewhat impatiently. “Now you must hold yourself firm to the end and not be so utterly cast down.”
> “I am thinking,” said Kristin, shivering, “of all those whom we harmed so that we might live to see this day—”
> “Nor have you had only good,” said Lady Aashild. “Not Erlend. And I think you have had it worse.”
> “I am thinking of his defenseless children,” said the bride as before. “I wonder whether they know that their father is celebrating his wedding today—”
> “Think of your own child,” said the lady. “Be glad that you are celebrating your wedding with the man who is its father.”
> Kristin lay there for a while, sinking and dizzy. It was so strange to hear named aloud what had filled her mind every single day for three months and more, though she had been unable to speak a word of it to a living soul. But it helped her only briefly.
> “I am thinking of her who had to pay with her life because she held Erlend dear,” she whispered, trembling.
> “You yourself may come to pay with your life before you are half a year older,” said Lady Aashild harshly. “Rejoice while you can—.
> “—What am I to say to you, Kristin?” said the old woman in exasperation. “Have you wholly lost courage now? Soon enough it will be demanded of you that you pay for everything you have taken—fear nothing else.”
> But Kristin felt as though avalanche after avalanche moved within her mind; everything she had built since that day of terror at Haugen was torn down. In the first time, when wildly and blindly she had thought only that she would endure, one day after another she would endure. And she had endured until it became easier—and easy at last, when she had cast away all thoughts but this: that now it would finally be their wedding, finally Erlend’s wedding.
> She and Erlend knelt together during the bridal Mass. But everything was like a deceptive vision—the candles, images, gleaming vessels, priests in linen vestments and choir copes. All the people who had known her in her former life—they seemed like dream visions as they stood filling the church in their unfamiliar holy-day clothes. But Sir Bjørn stood against a pillar and watched them with his dead eyes, and she thought the other dead woman must have returned with him, in his arm.
> She tried to look up at the image of Saint Olav—he stood red and white and fair, leaning upon his axe and trampling his own sinful human nature beneath his feet—but Sir Bjørn drew her gaze. And near him she saw Eline Ormsdatter’s dead face, looking at them indifferently. They had trampled over her to come here—and she did not begrudge them that.
> She had risen and shaken off all the stones Kristin had labored so hard to lay upon the dead woman. Erlend’s wasted youth, his honor and welfare, the favor of friends, the health of his soul. The dead woman shook it from herself. He wanted me and I wanted him; you wanted him and he wanted you, said Eline. I have paid, and he must pay, and you must pay when your time comes. When sin is perfected, then it brings forth death—.
> She thought she knelt with Erlend upon a cold stone. He knelt with the red, burned marks on his pale face; she knelt beneath the heavy bridal crown and felt the dull, crushing weight in her womb—the burden of sin she carried. She had played and frolicked with her sin, measured it as in a child’s game. Holy Virgin—soon the time would come when it lay full-borne before her, looked at her with living eyes, showed her the brands of sin, sin’s hideous deformity, struck hatefully with twisted hands against its mother’s breast.
> When she had borne her child, when she saw the marks of her sin upon it and loved it as she had loved her sin, then the game would be played to its end.
> Kristin thought that if she screamed now, so that it cut through the singing and the deep, chanting male voices—rang out over the people. Would she then be rid of Eline’s face—would life enter the dead man’s eyes? But she clenched her teeth.
> —Holy King Olav, I call to you. Among all in Heaven, I pray to you for help, because I know you loved God’s justice above all things. I call upon you to take charge of the innocent child in my mother’s womb. Turn God’s wrath from the innocent one, turn it upon me, amen, in the Lord’s precious name—.
> My children, said Eline, are surely innocent, and there is no place for them in a land where Christian people live. Your child was begotten in outlawry, like my children. You can claim no more right for it in the country you fled than I could claim for mine—.
> Holy Olav, I pray for mercy nevertheless. Pray for grace for my son, take him under your protection, and I shall carry him to your church on my bare feet; I shall carry my golden circlet to you and lay it upon your altar if you will help me, amen—.
> Her face was rigid as stone, so fiercely did she struggle to remain calm, but her body twitched and trembled as she knelt and was married to Erlend.
> And now she sat beside him in the high seat at home and perceived everything around her only as a feverish mirage.
> There were the entertainers playing harps and fiddles in the loft; there was playing and singing from the room below and outside in the courtyard. A gleam of red fire came from outside whenever they passed through the door carrying things in and out.
> They stood up around the table; she stood between her father and Erlend. Her father proclaimed in a loud voice that he had now given Erlend Nikulaussøn his daughter Kristin as wife. Erlend thanked his father-in-law and thanked all the good people who had gathered to honor him and his wife.
> They told her to sit down, and Erlend laid his bridal gifts in her lap. Sira Eirik and Sir Munan Baardsøn unrolled documents and read aloud concerning their common estate. Meanwhile the bridal attendants stood with spears in their hands and struck the shafts against the floor from time to time during the reading and whenever gifts and bags of money were laid upon the table.
> The loose tables that had been set in place were cleared away; Erlend led her onto the floor, and they danced. Kristin thought: our bridal attendants and maidens are far too young for us—all those who were young with us have moved away from these fields; how have we come back here?
> “You are so strange, Kristin,” Erlend whispered during the dance. “I grow afraid of you, Kristin—are you not glad—”
> They went from house to house and greeted their guests. Many candles burned in every room, and they were full of people drinking, singing, and dancing everywhere. Kristin felt she no longer recognized her home—and she had lost all sense of time—the hours and images floated strangely loose among one another.
> The autumn night was mild; there were musicians in the courtyard too, and people dancing around the bonfire. They shouted that the bride and bridegroom must honor them too, and she danced with Erlend in the cold, dew-wet courtyard. It was as though she awakened a little from this and her head grew clearer. Outside in the darkness a pale ribbon of mist floated over the rushing river. The mountains stood coal-black against the star-strewn air.
> Erlend led her out of the dance—crushed her to him in the darkness beneath a gallery.
> “I have not even had a chance to tell you—you are so fair, so fair and lovely. Your cheeks are as red as flames—” He pressed his cheek against hers as he spoke. “Kristin, what is the matter with you—?”
> “I am so tired, so tired,” she whispered back.
> “Soon we shall go and sleep,” replied the bridegroom, looking up at the sky. The Milky Way had turned and ran almost directly from south to north. “Do you remember that we have not slept together since the one night I was with you in your storehouse at Skog—”
> A while later Sira Eirik called across the courtyard that it was now Monday. The matrons came to lead the bride to bed—Kristin was so tired that she scarcely had strength to resist as she ought for seemliness’ sake. She allowed herself to be taken and led out of the loft by Lady Aashild and Gyrid of Skog. The bridal attendants stood at the foot of the stairs with burning tapers and drawn swords; they formed a ring around the company of women and accompanied Kristin across the courtyard and up into the old loft.
> The matrons removed her bridal finery, piece by piece, and put it away. Kristin saw that over the foot of the bed hung the violet-blue velvet gown she was to wear the next day, and upon it lay a long, snow-white, finely folded linen cloth. It was the wife’s linen Erlend had brought her; the next day she was to bind her hair into a knot and fasten it over it. It looked so fresh and cool and soothing.
> At last she stood before the bridal bed on bare feet, with bare arms, clothed only in the ankle-length, golden-yellow silk shift. They had placed the crown upon her again—the bridegroom was to remove it when the two were alone.
> Ragnfrid laid her hands upon her daughter’s shoulders and kissed her cheek—her mother’s face and hands were strangely cold, but it was as though weeping pressed from deep within her breast. Then she opened the bed and bade the bride sit down in it. Kristin obeyed and leaned back against the piled silk cushions at the headboard—she had to incline her head slightly forward to keep the crown in place. Lady Aashild covered her to the waist, laid her hands upon the silk spread, and took the bride’s shining hair, drawing it forward over her breast and her bare, slender upper arms.
> The men led the bridegroom into the loft. Munan Baardsøn unbuckled Erlend’s golden belt with the sword—when he hung it on the wall above the bed, he whispered something to the bride—Kristin did not understand what he said, but she smiled as well as she could.
> The bridal attendants unlaced Erlend’s silk garment and lifted the heavy, long robe over his head. He sat in the armchair, and they helped him remove his spurs and boots.
> Only once did the bride dare look up and meet his eyes.
> Then the good-night wishes began. The loft emptied of wedding guests. Last, Lavrans Bjørgulfsøn went out and closed the door of the bridal house.
> Erlend stood up, pulled off his underclothes, and flung them onto the bench. He stood before the bed, removed the crown and silk ribbons from her hair, and laid them on the table. Then he returned and climbed into the bed. Kneeling upon the bed beside her, he took hold of her head and pressed it against his naked, hot breast while kissing her forehead all along the red mark the crown had made.
> She threw her arms around his shoulders and sobbed aloud—sweetly and wildly she felt that now it faded away, the fear, the ghostly visions—now, now once again there were only he and she. He lifted her face for a moment, looked down into it, and passed a hand over her face and body, strangely swift and rough, as though tearing away a covering:
> “Forget,” he begged in a hot whisper, “forget everything, my Kristin—everything except that you are my own wife, and I am your own husband—”
> With his hand he struck out the last flame and cast himself down beside her in the darkness; he too sobbed:
> “I never believed it, never during all these years, that we should live to see this day—”
> Outside in the courtyard the commotion gradually quieted. Tired from the ride earlier that day and dizzy with drink, the guests wandered about a little longer for decency’s sake—but more and more slipped away and found the places where they were to sleep.
> Ragnfrid accompanied those who were to be most honored to their places and bade them good night. The master of the house, who should have helped her with this, was nowhere to be seen.
> Only a few small groups of young people—mostly servants—still stood in the dark courtyard when at last she slipped out to find her husband and bring him to bed. She had seen that Lavrans had grown thoroughly drunk as the evening passed.
> At last she stumbled over him while groping about outside the storehouse yard—he lay face downward in the grass behind the bathhouse.
> Feeling in the darkness, she recognized him—yes, it was he. She thought he was asleep, seized his shoulder, and tried to raise him from the ice-cold ground. But he was not asleep—not entirely, at any rate.
> “What do you want?” he asked thickly.
> “You cannot lie here,” said his wife. She supported him, for he stood swaying. With one hand she brushed him down over his velvet garment. “It is time we too went to bed, husband.” She took him beneath the arm and led the staggering man up toward the farm; they went along the rear of the storehouse buildings.
> “You did not look up, Ragnfrid, when you sat in the bridal bed beneath the crown,” he said as before. “Our daughter—she was less bashful; there was no bashfulness in her eyes when she looked at her bridegroom.”
> “She has waited for him for seven half-years,” said her mother softly. “Then she must surely dare look up—”
> “The devil they have waited,” screamed her father, and his wife hushed him fearfully.
> They were in the narrow lane between the rear of the privy and a split-rail fence. Lavrans struck his clenched fist against the lower log of the privy.
> “I laid you here for mockery and shame, you log. I laid you here so that filth might eat you up. I laid you here as punishment because you struck down my fair little maiden. I should have laid you above my loft door and honored and thanked you with beautiful carving because you saved her from shame and sorrow—because you caused my Ulvhild to die an innocent child—”
> He turned, staggered against the split-rail fence, and fell forward upon it, his head down on his arms; he wept uncontrollably, with long, deep groans between.
> His wife took hold of his shoulders:
> “Lavrans, Lavrans.” But she could not calm him. “Husband.”
> “Oh, never, never, never should I have given her to that man. God help me—I suppose I knew it all along—he had broken down her youth and her fair honor. I did not believe it—no, should I believe such a thing of Kristin—but still I knew. Even so, she is too good for that weak boy who has wasted himself and her—had he enticed her ten times, I should not have given her to him so that he can waste more of her life and happiness—”
> “What other course was there?” said her mother helplessly. “You understand too that she was already his—”
> “Yes, I need not have made such a great commotion to give Erlend what he had already taken for himself,” said Lavrans. “A noble husband my Kristin has gained—.” He tore at the split-rail fence. Then he wept again. Ragnfrid thought he had become a little more sober, but now the drink gained mastery again.
> As drunk and as utterly desperate as the man was, she did not think she could draw him up into the hearth-room where they were to sleep—it was full of guests. She looked around—nearby was a small barn where they stored the good hay for the horses during spring work. She went over and peered inside—no one lay there; then she led her husband in and closed the door behind them.
> Ragnfrid gathered hay around herself and him and covered them warmly with their cloaks. Lavrans wept from time to time and said something, but it was so confused that she could make no sense of it. After a while she lifted his head into her lap.
> “Dear husband—when they have bestowed such love upon each other—perhaps everything will be better than we think—”
> Lavrans replied in broken phrases—now seeming clearer again:
> “Do you not understand—he now has absolute power over her, he who has never been able to govern himself. It will be hard for her to find courage to oppose anything her husband desires—and if one day she must, then she will be so bitterly tormented by it herself—my gentle child—.
> “—Now I soon no longer understand why God lays so many heavy sorrows upon me—I have labored faithfully to do His will. Why has He taken our children from us, Ragnfrid, one after another—first our sons, then little Ulvhild, and now I have given away the one I held dearest, without honor, to an unreliable and foolish man. Now we have only the little one left, and it seems unwise to rejoice over her before I see how it fares with her, Ramborg.”
> Ragnfrid trembled like a leaf. Then the man put an arm around her shoulder:
> “Lie down,” he asked. “Then we shall sleep—” and with his head against his wife’s arm he lay for a while, sighing from time to time, but at last he fell asleep.
> It was still pitch-dark in the barn when Ragnfrid stirred—she wondered that she had slept. She felt before her with her hand; Lavrans sat upright with his hands clasped around his knees.
> “Are you awake already?” she asked in surprise. “Are you cold?”
> “No,” he replied, his voice rough, “but I cannot fall asleep again.”
> “Is it Kristin you are thinking of?” asked her mother. “It may fare better than we think, Lavrans,” she said again.
> “Yes, that is what I am thinking of,” said the man. “Well, well. Maiden or woman, she came to the bridal bed with the one upon whom she had bestowed her love. Neither you nor I did that, my poor Ragnfrid.”
> His wife gave a dull, deep groan—she flung herself sideways into the hay. Lavrans moved one hand onto her shoulder.
> “But I could not,” he said vehemently and painfully. “No, I could not—be as you wished me to be—when we were young. I am not like that—”
> After a while Ragnfrid said softly, weeping:
> “Still, we have lived well together—Lavrans—all these years.”
> “So I myself believed,” he replied darkly.
> Thoughts tumbled and pressed within him. The one naked glance bridegroom and bride had exchanged, the two young faces that flamed up in red fire—to him it seemed shamelessness. It had seared him; this was his daughter. But he continued seeing those eyes—and wildly and blindly he resisted having the covering torn away from something in his own heart that he had never wished to know was there, which he had defended against his own wife when she sought it—.
> He had not been able, he insisted harshly to himself. In the devil’s name, he had been married off as a boy; he had not chosen for himself; she was older than he—he had not desired her; he had not wished to learn this from her—love. He still grew hot with shame when he thought of it—that she had wanted him to love her when he had not wanted such love from her. That she had offered him all this which he had never asked for.
> He had been a good husband to her; so he himself had believed. He had shown her all the honor he could, given her full authority with him and sought her counsel in everything, been faithful to her—and they had had six children. He had merely wished to be allowed to live with her without her always grasping for that in his heart which he himself would not lay bare—.
> He had borne love for no one.—Ingunn, Karl’s wife at Bru. Lavrans blushed in the pitch darkness. He had always visited them when he traveled down through the valley. He had surely never even spoken with the mistress alone once. But when he saw her—merely when he thought of her, he sensed something like the first smell of the fields in spring, just after the snow has gone. He knew it now: it could have happened to him too—he too could have loved.
> But he had been married so young, and he had grown shy. Thus he had come to thrive best out in the lonely forest—upon the upland, where every living creature wants broad space around itself, space through which to flee—shyly they watch every stranger who tries to creep upon them—.
> At one time of year the animals in forest and mountain forgot their shyness. Then they displayed themselves before their females. But he had been given his. And she had offered him everything for which he had not sued—.
> But the young in the nest.—They had been the little warm spot in the wilderness, the innermost sweetest delight of his life. The little fair heads beneath his hand.—
> Married, he had become—almost without being asked. Friends—he had many, and he had none. War—that had been joy, but there was no more war—his armor hung upstairs in the loft, little used. He had become a farmer—. But he had had his daughters—everything he had lived had become dear because through it he secured them, the fine, soft little lives he had held in his hands. He remembered Kristin’s little two-year-old body upon his shoulder, her flax-pale, soft hair against his cheek. Her small hands holding his belt while she butted her round, hard child’s forehead against his shoulder blades when he rode out with her behind him on the horse.
> And now she had those same hot eyes—and she had gained what was hers. She sat there in the half-shadow against the silk cushions of the bed. In the candlelight she was entirely golden—golden crown and golden shift and golden hair over the naked golden arms. Her eyes were no longer bashful—.
> Her father writhed with shame.
> And yet it was as though his heart burst open bleeding.
> For what he himself had not received. And for the wife here at his side, which he had not been able to give.
> Sick with pity, he reached in the darkness for Ragnfrid’s hand:
> “Yes, I believed we lived well together,” he said. “I thought you grieved for our children.—Yes, and that you had a heavy disposition. Never did I think it might be I who was not a good husband to you—”
> Ragnfrid shook as though in convulsions:
> “You were always a good husband, Lavrans.”
> “Hmm.” Lavrans sat with his chin all the way down on his knees. “Even so, perhaps you would have fared better if you had married as our daughter did today—”
> Ragnfrid sprang up and screamed low and piercingly:
> “You know! How did you learn—how long have you known—”
> “I do not know what you are speaking of,” said Lavrans after a while, in a strangely crushed voice.
> “I am speaking of this: that I was not a maiden when I became your wife,” replied Ragnfrid, and her voice was clear and ringing with despair.
> After a while Lavrans replied as before:
> “I never knew that until now.”
> Ragnfrid lay down in the hay, shaken with weeping. When the fit was over, she lifted her head a little. Some gray light had begun to seep through the opening in the wall. She could dimly see her husband sitting with his arms entwined around his knees, motionless as though made of stone.
> “Lavrans—speak to me—” she lamented.
> “What do you want me to say?” he asked without moving.
> “Oh, I do not know—that you should curse me—strike me—”
> “It would be somewhat late now,” replied the man; there was something like the shadow of a mocking smile in his voice.
> Ragnfrid wept again. “I did not consider then that I was betraying you. I thought I myself had been so betrayed and so violated. No one had spared me. They came with you—I had seen you only three times before we were married—I thought you were merely a boy, red and white—so young and childish—”
> “I was,” said Lavrans, and there was a little more resonance in his voice. “And therefore I would have thought that you, who were a woman, would have been more afraid to—to betray one who was so young that he understood nothing—”
> “So I came to think later,” said Ragnfrid, weeping. “When I had come to know you. Soon enough came the time when I would have given my soul twenty times over if I could have become guiltless toward you.”
> Lavrans sat silent and motionless; then his wife said again:
> “You ask nothing?”
> “What use would it be? It was he whose—funeral procession we met at Feginsbrekka when we carried Ulvhild into Nidaros—”
> “Yes,” said Ragnfrid. “We had to leave the road—go into the meadow. I saw them carry his bier past—with priests and monks and armed retainers. I heard he had received a good death—reconciled with God. As we stood with Ulvhild’s bier between us, I prayed that my sin and my sorrow might be laid at his feet on the Last Day—”
> “Yes, you surely did,” said Lavrans, and the same shadow of mockery lay in his quiet voice.
> “You do not know everything,” said Ragnfrid, cold with despair. “Do you remember that he came out to us at Skog during the first winter we were married—”
> “Yes,” replied the man.
> “When Bjørgulf lay struggling with death—. Oh, no one had spared me. He was drunk when he did it to me—afterward he said he had never held me dear; he did not want me—he asked me to forget it—. My father did not know this; he did not betray you, you must never think that. But Trond—we were dearest friends then; I complained of it to him. He wished to threaten that man into marrying me—but he was only a boy, and he was beaten—. Afterward he advised me to keep silent and take you—”
> She sat still for a while.
> “When he came out to Skog—. A year had passed; I did not think so much about it. But he came there—he said he repented; he would have had me then if I had been unmarried—he loved me. So he said. God must judge whether he spoke truth. When he had left—I did not dare go onto the fjord; I did not dare because of the sin, not with the child. And then I had—then I had begun to love you so!” She screamed once, as in the wildest torment. Her husband quickly turned his head toward her.
> “When Bjørgulf was born—oh, I thought I loved him more than my life. When he lay struggling with death—I thought, if he goes out, then I too shall go out. But I did not pray that God spare the boy’s life—”
> Lavrans sat for a very long time before asking, dully and heavily:
> “Was that because I was not his father?”
> “I did not know whether you were,” said Ragnfrid, stiffening.
> For a long time they both sat deathly still. Then the man suddenly asked vehemently:
> “In Jesus’ name, Ragnfrid—why do you tell me this—now?”
> “Oh, I do not know.” She twisted her hands until the finger joints cracked. “So that you may take revenge on me. Drive me from your farm—”
> “Do you think that would help me—?” His voice trembled with mockery. “Then there are our daughters,” he said quietly. “Kristin—and the little one.”
> Ragnfrid sat for a while.
> “I remember how you judged Erlend Nikulaussøn,” she said softly. “What judgment do you pass upon me, then—”
> A long shudder passed through the man’s body—loosening a little of his rigidity.
> “You have now—we have now lived together for nearly twenty-seven years. It is not as with a strange man. I understand that you have had it worse than hard.”
> Ragnfrid collapsed sobbing beneath his words. She dared reach for one of his hands. He did not move, sitting still as a dead man. Then she wept louder and louder—her husband sat just as motionless and looked toward the gray light around the door. At last she lay as though all her tears had run out.
> Then he stroked her arm fleetingly. At once she began to weep again.
> “Do you remember,” she said between sobs, “the man who once came to us while we were at Skog? The one who knew the ancient lays? Do you remember one about a dead man who had returned from the world of torment and told his son of what he had seen? Then a din sounded from hell’s deepest ground: faithless women ground earth for food for their husbands. Bloody were the stones they pulled; bloody hung their hearts from their breasts—”
> Lavrans said nothing.
> “I have thought of those words throughout all these years,” said Ragnfrid. “Every day it was as though my heart bled, for every day I thought I was grinding earth for you to eat—”
> Lavrans did not himself know why he answered as he did. He felt hollow and empty in the breast like a man upon whose back they had carved the blood eagle. But he laid his hand heavily and wearily upon his wife’s head and said:
> “Earth must surely be ground, my Ragnfrid, before food can grow.”
> When she tried to take his hand and kiss it, he snatched it violently away. Then he looked down at his wife, took one of her hands and laid it upon his knee, and bowed his cold, stiffened face toward it. And thus they remained sitting, without moving and without speaking further to one another.
